Thermodynamic Formalism for Quasimorphisms: Bounded Cohomology and Statistics
Pablo D. Carrasco, Federico Rodríguez-Hertz

Abstract
. For a compact negatively curved space, we develop a thermodynamic formalism framework to study the space of quasimorphisms of its fundamental group modulo bounded functions. We prove that this space is Banach isomorphic to the space of Bowen functions on the associated Gromov geodesic flow, modulo a weak form of Livšic cohomology. We also show that each unbounded quasimorphism is associated with a unique invariant measure for the flow, which uniquely determines the cohomology class. As a consequence, we establish the Central Limit Theorem and the invariance principle for any unbounded quasimorphism with respect to Markov measures, and we prove that the associated equilibrium state has the Bernoulli property.



1.
Introduction and main results

In this article we study the bounded cohomology of compact spaces of negative curvature, and its counterpart on the corresponding fundamental group. The theory of bounded cohomology was introduced by Johnson in the context of Banach algebras
[
1
]
, but gained maturity with the foundational paper of Gromov
[
2
]
, where it was extended to topological spaces. For a topological space
M
, one considers the usual singular cochain complex
\{C^{n}(M;\mathbb{R}),\partial_{n}\}
and observes that the boundary map
\partial
sends bounded cochains in
C^{n}(M;\mathbb{R})
to bounded cochains in
C^{n+1}(M;\mathbb{R})
. The bounded cohomology of
M
is the cohomology of the cochain complex
\{C_{b}^{n}(M;\mathbb{R}),\partial_{n}\}
and is denoted by
H_{\mathrm{b}}^{*}(M;\mathbb{R})
. For a discrete group
\Gamma
, its bounded cohomology can be defined as
H^{*}_{\mathrm{b}}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}):=H^{*}_{\mathrm{b}}(K(\Gamma,1);\mathbb{R})
. In addition to its algebraic structure, the vector space
H^{*}_{\mathrm{b}}(M,\mathbb{R})
carries a natural seminorm,
\lVert[f]\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}\coloneqq\inf\{\lVert g\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}:[g]=[f]\}
.

Banach Structure of

\bm{\Ker c_{2}}

Of particular interest is the second bounded cohomology group
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})
, and the kernel of the map
c_{2}:H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(M;\mathbb{R})
induced by the inclusion
C^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow C^{2}(M,\mathbb{R})
. This map is called the comparison map. If
\pi_{1}(M,*)
is (word) hyperbolic then
c_{2}
is surjective, and in fact it is a result of Mineyev
[
3
]
that hyperbolicity of a group is equivalent to surjectivity of the comparison map replacing
\mathbb{R}
by arbitrary bounded
G
-modules. Moreover, by the work of Matsumoto and Morita
[
4
]
, and independently Ivanov
[
5
]
, it follows that the natural seminorm on
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})
is positive definite, hence
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})
is a Banach space. In this setting however, not much is known about the Banach structure besides the fact that
\Ker c_{2}
is infinite dimensional. This was originally established by Brooks and Series
[
6
]
for surface groups (see also Mitsumatsu
[
7
]
, Barge and Ghys
[
8
]
), extended for non-elementary word hyperbolic groups by Epstein and Fujiwara
[
9
]
, and then pushed further for non-elementary countable subgroups of the isometry group of a Gromov hyperbolic metric space, assuming some properness condition of the action (Fujiwara
[
10
]
, then Bestvina and Fujiwara
[
11
]
, culminating with Hamenstäedt
[
12
]
). We point out that these constructions fall into two (essentially equivalent) types, either they directly construct an infinite linearly independent subset in
\Ker c_{2}
, or they inject linearly some infinite dimensional vector space. In both, no information is given about the norm. Understanding the Banach structure of
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M;\mathbb{R})
and obtaining a concrete characterization of it is one of the main motivations of this paper.

It is well known that elements of
\Ker c_{2}
can be represented by quasimorphisms of
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
, that is, functions
L:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\lVert\delta L\rVert\coloneqq\sup_{g,h}|L(g\cdot h)-L(g)-L(h)|<\infty
: see
Section

3
for details. For the present discussion it suffices to say that the space
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
of quasimorphisms of
\Gamma
is a vector space containing both the bounded functions on
\Gamma
and the real-valued morphisms of
\Gamma
(denoted by
\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma),\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
, respectively). Moreover, the seminorm
L\mapsto\lVert\delta L\rVert
induces a norm on

\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})\oplus\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)},

and with the latter this space is isometric to
\Ker(c_{2})
.

Unbounded Cohomology

It will be convenient (and more natural) to ignore the purely algebraic part
\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
and work with

\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)}.

Definition 1.1
.

The space
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
will be referred to as the space of unbounded quasimorphisms on
\Gamma
.

The seminorm on quasimorphisms does not induce a norm on
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, hence some care is needed. We proceed as follows: assume that
\Gamma
is finitely generated (which is the case of interest for us), fix some finite generating set
S
, and define

\lVert L\rVert=\lVert L|S\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}+\lVert\delta L\rVert.

This defines a complete norm on
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, which in turn induces a Banach structure on
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, and moreover induces the same norm on
\Ker c_{2}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\,\mathbb{R})}
as before. This does not seem to be readily available in the literature, so we include a discussion in
Section

3
.

Unbounded classes as probabilities

In this article (and its sequel) we propose a different way to understand classes of (un)bounded cohomology. We will show that for some natural classes of groups
\Gamma
, each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is uniquely determined by a probability measure which is related to a natural dynamical system associated to the group. In this article we focus on the hyperbolic case.

Our results apply to compact negatively curved spaces and some of them even apply to non-elementary hyperbolic groups, but since the formulation of the theorems in these contexts requires the introduction of additional dynamical concepts, we restrict ourselves in the introduction to the following case. Let
M
be a
d-
dimensional closed hyperbolic manifold, and let
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}:X=T^{1}M\to X
be the geodesic flow associated with the metric.

The aforementioned probability measures in this case turn out to be equilibrium measures for some non-regular potentials. To define these potentials we fix some reference measure on
X
which is invariant under
\mathfrak{g}
. For concreteness, we work with
\mu_{X}
, the Liouville probability measure on
X
.

Definition 1.2
.

A Borel function
\phi\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu_{X}\right)
is a weak Bowen function if there exist
C,\varepsilon>0
so that
(\mu_{X}\text{-a.e.}\ x,y\in X,\forall T>0):

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\distop_{X}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq\varepsilon\Rightarrow\left\lvert\int_{0}^{T}\phi(g_{t}x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\phi(g_{t}y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert\leq C.

The Bowen constant of
\phi
is
\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\inf C
.

Functions satisfying this condition were introduced by Bowen in his studies of the thermodynamic formalism of hyperbolic systems
[
13
]
, and received a lot of attention since then. See e.g.
[
14
]
,
[
15
]
and references therein. Bowen’s definition, however, deals with (typically continuous) functions defined everywhere, and not almost everywhere with respect to some fully supported measure. This difference will be key in what follows.

We denote by
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
the vector space of weak Bowen functions for some a priori fixed
\varepsilon>0
. Note that since
\mathfrak{g}
is an Anosov flow
[
16
]
, it follows without much trouble that
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
contains the space of Hölder continuous functions on
X
.

Livšic cohomology

It will be shown that each
\varphi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
has an associated
\mathfrak{g}-
invariant probability measure
\mu_{\varphi}
, and furthermore
\mu_{\varphi}=\mu_{\varphi^{\prime}}
if and only if
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
satisfy a cohomology relation that we define below. Ultimately, classes in
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
will correspond to cohomology classes of weak Bowen functions.

Definition 1.3
.

We say that
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
are Livšic cohomologous (
\phi\sim\psi
) if
\exists u\in L^{\infty}\!\left(X,\mu_{X}\right)
which is differentiable along orbits of
\mathfrak{g}
, such that
\phi-\psi=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\circ g_{t}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}|_{t=0}
.

The above definition is well known in ergodic theory, and its importance owes much to the work of Livšic
[
17
]
, especially for regular (Hölder) functions. An in-depth analysis of this equivalence relation for the general case that we are discussing is carried out in
Section

4
. In particular, one has that
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)/\thicksim
is naturally a Banach space with respect to the norm

(1.1)

\displaystyle\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert[\phi]\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\inf\{\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}:\psi\sim\phi\}.

There is a natural involution
I:X\to X
which reverses the direction of geodesics. We say that
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
is antisymmetric if
\phi\circ I
is Livšic cohomologous to
-\phi
, and denote by

\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)

the set of antisymmetric weak Bowen functions.

Antisymmetry arises naturally in our description of quasimorphisms. In contrast with
[
12
]
, where a related anti-flip condition appears as part of a specific construction, it serves here to characterize the image of the correspondence. This is formalized in the following theorem.

Theorem A
.

Let
M
be a closed hyperbolic manifold with fundamental group
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
. Then there is a Banach isomorphism

\Psi:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)/\thicksim.

Remark 1.4
.

We say that a probability measure
\mu\in{P}(X)
is invariant under the flow
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
if
\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\mu=\mu\circ g_{t}
. The set of invariant measures under
\mathfrak{g}
is denoted by
{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
.

The above theorem remains true if
\mu_{X}
is replaced by any other invariant measure
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
of full support.

Concrete representation of

\bm{\Ker c_{2}}

This implies almost directly that
\Ker c_{2}
has uncountable dimension (a fact already known from the previously cited works), but more importantly, it elucidates its Banach space structure. Let us recall some alternative characterization due to Monod and Shalom
[
18
]
, based on the seminal work of Burger and Monod
[
19
]
.

A standard Borel
\Gamma
-space
(B,\nu)
(where
\nu
is quasi-invariant) is a strong boundary of
\Gamma
if

•

\Gamma\curvearrowright B
is amenable in the Zimmer sense
[
20
]
,

•

if
E
is a separable coefficient
\Gamma-
module, then every measurable
\Gamma-
invariant map
\Gamma\times B\to E
is
\nu-
essentially constant.

Theorem 1.5

(Corollary
2.6
in
[
18
]
)
.

If
\Gamma
is a countable discrete group and
(B,\nu)
is a strong boundary for it, then there exists a canonical isomorphism
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(\Gamma)\cong ZL^{\infty}_{\mathrm{a}}(B^{3})^{\Gamma}
, where the right-hand side is the space of
\Gamma-
invariant, alternating
\nu-
essentially bounded real-valued
2-
cocycles on
B
.

For
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
with
M
compact hyperbolic manifold, one can choose
B=\partial\widetilde{M}
, identified as the Poisson boundary associated to some non-degenerate random walk on
\Gamma
, say for a driving measure with finite first moment
[
21
]
.

The equivalence
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(\Gamma)\cong ZL^{\infty}_{\mathrm{a}}(\partial M^{3})^{\Gamma}
, although conceptually important, replaces
H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(\Gamma)
by another complicated Banach space, which is difficult to deal with. In contrast, the equivalence given in
Theorem

A
replaces
2-
cocycles with equivalence classes of functions, which we will show to be more manageable objects. The principal tool for this is a measurable version of Livšic theorem which we prove in
Section

5
, stating that Livšic cohomology classes of elements in
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
are completely determined by their values on closed geodesics. To be more precise, each closed geodesic
\gamma
in
X
defines a linear functional
P_{\gamma}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\varphi\sim\phi
if and only if for every
\gamma
one has
P_{\gamma}(\varphi)=P_{\gamma}(\phi)
. For a continuous weak Bowen function
\varphi
,
P_{\gamma}(\varphi)
coincides with the average of
\varphi
along
\gamma
. However, for general weak Bowen functions the theorem is much more delicate, since in principle
\varphi
is only an essentially bounded function on
X
, and
\mu_{X}(\gamma)=0
, so one cannot evaluate
\varphi
along the curve.

We point out that the classical version of Livsic cohomology (for Hölder potentials) is insufficient for our purposes, as there are examples of continuous functions that are not continuously cohomologous (meaning, with continuous transfer function
u
) to any Hölder function. See
Remark

5.26
.

All (un)bounded cohomology classes

Some authors have previously obtained related characterizations of
\Ker c_{2}
using dynamical systems. The first was probably Picaud in the setting where
M
is a hyperbolic surface
[
22
]
, who proved that one can embed
\Ker c_{2}
into the space of
\Gamma
-invariant measures on
\partial M
, or alternatively, into the space of geodesic currents in the sense of Bonahon and Sullivan. Then, using symbolic dynamics for the action of
\Gamma
on
\partial M

[
23
]
, he characterized some regular quasimorphisms
1
1

1

Essentially, homogeneous quasimorphisms that vanish on the set of generators of
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
as Hölder functions on a subshift of finite type. The arguments rely heavily on the two-dimensionality of
M
(although they also work for surfaces of finite type).

However, the restriction on the class of quasimorphisms considered is more serious. On the one hand, there are no general methods available to check whether a given quasimorphism corresponds to a Hölder function, while on the other hand not every quasimorphism corresponds to such functions.

Restricting to Hölder classes of objects also appears in the work of Hamenstäedt
[
12
]
, who showed that (antisymmetric) Hölder functions endowed with the seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
continuously embed into
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, and in other places e.g.
[
24
]
.

Theorem

A
makes it possible to consider all quasimorphisms. We will apply this to obtain precise statistical properties of these objects.

Unbounded quasimorphisms as invariant measures

The philosophy that drives the previous result is that in fact elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
can be understood as a certain type of
\mathfrak{g}-
invariant measures, namely equilibrium states for weak Bowen functions. The perspective of treating quasimorphisms as dynamically defined measures appears to be new, at least in explicit form (see, however,
[
22
]
), and proves to be useful. In
[
25
]
, we adopt this approach to provide a new proof of a beautiful result of Burger and Monod, which states that if
\Gamma
is a uniform lattice in a real semisimple Lie group of non-compact type and rank at least
2
, then
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)={0}
.

Each
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
defines a positive linear functional on
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)
, which extends the usual integral for continuous functions with the Bowen property, and therefore is denoted in the same manner. Below, if
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
, then
h_{\mu}(\mathfrak{g})=h_{\mu}(g_{1})
denotes the metric entropy of the time-one map
g_{1}
with respect to
\mu
.

Theorem B
.

Let
M
be a closed hyperbolic manifold. For each
[\phi]\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)/\thicksim
there exists a measure
\mu_{[\phi]}=\mu_{\phi}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
which can be characterized as the unique element of this space satisfying

h_{\mu_{\phi}}\!\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)+\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\phi}=\sup_{\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)}\left(h_{\mu}\!\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)+\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\right).

For every
t\neq 0
, the stochastic process defined by
(\phi\circ g_{nt})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}:X\to\mathbb{R}
is measure-theoretically isomorphic to a Bernoulli process with finitely many symbols.

We will show that
\mu_{[\phi]}
uniquely determines
[\phi]\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{X}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)/\thicksim
, hence by
Theorem

A
, the unique associated unbounded quasimorphism (provided that
\phi
is antisymmetric).

Remark 1.6
.

Again, one can replace the Liouville measure by any other fixed invariant measure of full support.

The theory of thermodynamic formalism for non-continuous potentials was first developed by Haydn and Ruelle in
[
26
]
(see also Ruelle’s article
[
27
]
). These works are mainly concerned with the existence of dominant eigenfunctions and eigenmeasures for the corresponding transfer operator, but finer (as well as cohomological) properties of the systems were not addressed. A plausible reason for this is that the arguments involve some arbitrary extensions of the potential, which makes further investigations difficult. Here we give a different concrete construction for weak Bowen functions, but of course we have been inspired by these previous works.

Central Limit Theorem for quasimorphisms

Using the characterization given by
Theorem

A
we are able to show that any non-trivial element of
\Ker(c_{2})
has a very rich dynamical behavior.

Theorem C
.

Let
M
be a closed hyperbolic manifold. Then there exists a Markov measure
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)
such that the following is true. For any
0\neq[\phi]\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(X)/\thicksim
there exists a positive constant
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}([\phi])
so that for
\mathbb{E}[[\phi]]=\mathbb{E}[\phi]=\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
, it holds

\forall c\in\mathbb{R},\ \mu\left(x\in X:\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\phi(g_{t}x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-T\mathbb{E}[\phi]}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

Central Limit Theorems for quasimorphisms were first studied by Calegari and Fujiwara in
[
28
]
, and have been thoroughly studied ever since. For a gentle introduction we refer to the notes of Calegari
[
29
]
. See also Horsham and Sharp
[
24
]
, and the more recent article of Cantrell
[
30
]
.

Unlike
Theorem

B
, the cited limit theorems are given with respect to some appropriate exit measure for a random walk in the group (for example, the Patterson-Sullivan measure). But more importantly, the most general version
[
30
]
assumes a strong form of regularity, essentially that the quasimorphism corresponds to a Hölder continuous function on the subshift associated to some Strong Markov structure of
\Gamma
.

To treat all unbounded cohomology classes of quasimorphisms we prove a general version of the Central Limit Theorem for discrete Markov chains, which to our knowledge is new even in the independent case. The novelty is that we can consider highly non-regular processes given by measurable functions on the corresponding subshift (weak Bowen functions). Observe that in this level of generality even the convergence of the variance for such functions is non-trivial. On the other hand, the method does not use any spectral gap property for the action of the Ruelle-Perron-Frobenius operator on the space of Bowen functions (which is probably not true), and we do not obtain any Barry-Esseen type bound.

Random walks and quasimorphisms

Let us state the previous theorem directly in the group context. For a closed hyperbolic manifold
M
, write as above
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
, and let
\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)=\Gamma/\Inn(\Gamma)
denote its set of conjugacy classes. Equip
\Gamma
with a word metric associated to some finite set of generators
S
.
\Gamma
is word hyperbolic, therefore any
\gamma\in\Gamma
can be represented as a word in
S
of minimal length, and two such words differ by a cyclic permutation. For our purposes it is more convenient to work with
\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)
, the set of reduced cyclic words of
\Gamma
in
S
, instead of
\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)
. If
w\in\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)
let
\lvert w\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}
denote its length.

We say that
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
are cohomologous if
[L]=[L^{\prime}]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
. Every quasimorphism
L
is cohomologous to a class function
\overline{L}
: as a result, the asymptotic behavior of
L
on conjugacy classes can be studied via the corresponding analysis of
\overline{L}:\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)\to\mathbb{R}
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
B_{n}\coloneqq\{w\in\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S):\lvert w\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}\leq n\}
, which is a finite set, and consider the probability measures

\nu_{n}^{\Gamma,S}:=\frac{1}{\#B_{n}}\sum_{w\in B_{n}}\delta_{w}.

Ideally, one would like to use a limiting distribution of
(\nu_{n}^{\Gamma,S})_{n}
to study the statistical properties of
\bar{L}
, but since
\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)
lacks sufficient structure, it is necessary to enlarge it in order to find this distribution.

Theorem 1.7

(Compactification of conjugacy classes)
.

There exists a compact metric space
X_{\Gamma}
, a filtration of finite
\sigma-
algebras
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n}
of
X_{\Gamma}
, an injective map
\Phi:\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)\to X_{\Gamma}
, a constant
D\geq 0
and a probability
\mu^{\Gamma}\in{P}(X_{\Gamma})
satisfying:

(1)

\mathrm{Im}(\Phi)
is dense in
X_{\Gamma}
;

(2)

\Phi(w)\in\mathcal{F}_{D+|w|_{S}}
;

(3)

the sequence
(\mu_{n}^{\Gamma,S}=\nu_{n}^{\Gamma,S}\circ\Phi^{-1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
\mu^{\Gamma}
.

We remark that
\mu_{n}^{\Gamma,S}
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{F}_{D+n}
. If
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, one first considers the induced function
\bar{L}:\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)\to\mathbb{R}
, and then uses
\Phi
as above to induce a sequence of continuous functions
(L^{(n)}:X_{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{R})_{n}
which verifies

(1)

L^{(n)}(\Psi(w))=\overline{L}(w)
, if
\lvert w\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=n
;

(2)

L^{(n)}
is
\mathcal{F}_{n}-
measurable.

See
4.2
for details. The measure
\mu^{\Gamma}
does not depend on chosen generating set
S
. On the other hand, the construction of
(L^{(n)})_{n}
is not canonical, but can be achieved so that two of such sequences are at uniformly bounded distance from each other. Thus, with no loss of generality, we assume that
L
itself is a class function.

Corollary C
.

Let
M
be a closed hyperbolic manifold with fundamental group
\Gamma
. If
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is unbounded, then there exist constants
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}(L)>0,e(L)\in\mathbb{R}
so that for every
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mu^{\Gamma}\left(x\in X_{\Gamma}:\frac{L^{(n)}(x)-ne(L)}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

In
[
31
]
Björklund and Harnick prove a very general Central Limit Theorem for quasimorphisms on locally countable groups when the sequence of words is chosen by a random walk whose driving measure
\mu
is spread-out. When the corresponding stationary measure on
\partial\Gamma
is the Patterson-Sullivan measure, the results in
[
32
]
imply that the CLT holds for every quasimorphism with respect to
\mu
.

However, these results concern distributions arising from random walks on the group. In contrast, the measure
\mu^{\Gamma}
considered here, although reminiscent of the Patterson-Sullivan measure, is not associated to a random walk on
\Gamma
. Instead, it arises as a uniform measure on a natural compactification of
\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)
. The two settings are conceptually different and consequently, the results of
[
31
,
32
]
do not imply
Corollary

C
. The latter permits one to study the distribution of quasimorphisms on conjugacy classes, which provides a natural framework for the problem.

More generally, the results above show that quasimorphisms admit a natural dynamical
and probabilistic interpretation, allowing their statistical properties to be studied
using tools from thermodynamic formalism.

Functional limit theorem, Law of iterated logarithm and large deviations

Other limit theorems follow from our approach. We highlight the invariance principle, the law of the iterated logarithm, and concentration inequalities, giving a unified treatment of these results in full generality.

If
(J_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a stationary sequence of zero mean in
(X_{\Gamma},\mu^{\Gamma})
, let
S_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}J_{k}
and consider the random element
\mathfrak{L}:\mathbb{N}\times X_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{C}([0,1])

\mathfrak{L}_{t}(n,x)=\left(\mathfrak{L}(n,x)\right)(t)=\frac{S_{([nt])}(x)+(nt-[nt])J_{[nt]+1}(x)}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\quad t\in[0,1].

Let
\eta_{n}=\mu^{\Gamma}\circ(\mathfrak{L}(n,\cdot))^{-1}
be its distribution. Define

\mathcal{K}=\{x\in\mathcal{C}([0,1]):x(0)=0,x\text{ is absolutely continuous, and }\int_{0}^{1}\dot{x}^{2}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq 1\}.

For
e<t<+\infty
denote
\phi(t)=\sqrt{2t\log\log t}
.

Theorem D
.

Under the hypothesis of the previous corollary, suppose that
L
is unbounded with
e(L)=0
. Then, there exists a stationary ergodic sequence
(J_{n})_{n\geq 1}
so that

(1)

\sup_{n\geq 1,x\in X_{\Gamma}}|S_{n}(x)-L^{(n)}(x)|<\infty
;

(2)

the sequence of distributions
(\eta_{n})_{n}
converges weakly to the Wiener measure. That is,
(\mathfrak{L}_{t}(n,\cdot))_{n}
converges in distribution to the standard Brownian process.

(3)

For
\mu^{\Gamma}\text{-a.e.}\ x
, the sequence
\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}(n,x)}{\phi(n\sigma^{2}(L))}\right)_{n\geq\frac{e}{\sigma^{2}(L)}}
is relatively compact, and each of its limit points belongs to
\mathcal{K}
.

(4)

For every
0\leq\delta\leq 1
there exists
H(\delta)\in(0,1],C>0
such that for every
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mu^{\Gamma}\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{n}\geq\delta\right)\leq e^{-nH(\delta)}

\displaystyle\mu^{\Gamma}\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{\sqrt{n}}\geq\delta\right)\leq e^{-C\delta^{2}}.

As in
Theorem

C
, this will be a consequence of the corresponding statements for weak Bowen functions. We remark that for regular (edge-combable) quasimorphisms
[
30
]
gives a large deviation theorem and, although not explicitly stated, the results in
[
33
]
can be adapted to give the invariance principle and the LIL for these regular quasimorphisms.

Asymptotics with respect to the Patterson-Sullivan measure

When
\Gamma
is a non-elementary Gromov-hyperbolic group, as in the case that we are discussing, there exists a symbolic coding of
\partial\Gamma
(the so-called Cannon coding), which allows one to lift the Patterson-Sullivan measure to a Markov measure. It was kindly pointed out to us by S. Cantrell that, combining this with the technique developed for proving
Theorem

C
, one obtains remarkable corollaries about the asymptotics of any unbounded quasimorphism with respect to the Patterson-Sullivan measure, and with respect to averaging on spheres in the Cayley graph.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
denote
S_{n}=\{g\in\Gamma:\lvert g\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=n\}
the sphere of radius
n
. For a ray
r\subset\mathrm{Cay}(\Gamma)
we write
r_{n}\in S_{n}
for the initial segment of size
n
. The class determined by
r
on
\partial\Gamma
is denoted by
[r]
. The identity of
\Gamma
is
1_{\Gamma}
.

Corollary D
.

Let
\Gamma
be a non-elementary Gromov-hyperbolic group, and let
\nu\in{P}(\partial\Gamma)
be the Patterson-Sullivan measure. If
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is unbounded, then there exist
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}(L)>0
such that for every
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\nu\left([\tilde{r}]:\exists r\in[\tilde{r}]\text{ with }r_{0}=1_{\Gamma}\text{ and }\frac{L(r_{n})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#S_{n}}\#\left\{g\in S_{n}:\frac{L(g)}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right\}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

We remark that this corollary does not follow from
[
31
]
, which concerns limit theorems for quasimorphisms with respect to the driving measure of a random walk on the group. In contrast,
Corollary

D
describes the asymptotic behavior with respect to the Patterson-Sullivan measure on the boundary.

On the definition of Dynamical System

At the end of this introduction, we would like to draw the reader’s attention to some interpretations of the meaning of the previous theorems from the optics of dynamical systems. The notion of classical dynamical systems, used by Poincaré, Gibbs, Boltzmann and many others at that time, consists of (at least) two main components: the law of evolution, which is modeled for example by some flow
(g_{t}:M\to M)_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on the phase space, and an observable, which is represented by some function
\phi:M\to\mathbb{R}
. The evolution is in principle unknown, and the only possible interaction with the physical system is to take measurements at discrete times. To smooth out small fluctuations, it is more natural to record
\phi^{(n)}:M\to\mathbb{R}
, the cumulative sum of the measurements up to time
n
, and then take the average. In this way, what one actually gets is a sequence
\{\phi^{(n)}:M\to\mathbb{R}\}_{n\geq 0}
which, assuming that measurement errors remain bounded (otherwise no relevant information is extracted), satisfies some condition of the form

\sup_{n,m}|\phi^{(n+m)}-\phi^{(n)}-\phi^{(m)}\circ g_{n}|<C<\infty.

In this interpretation, quasimorphisms play the role of approximate observables whose cumulative measurements satisfy the bounded error relation above. What
Theorems

A
,
B
and
C
and
Theorem

D
say is that if the driving evolution is sufficiently chaotic, then the system has an error-correcting mechanism that allows the observable
\phi
to be recovered, at least for almost every point, and in such a way that the empirical measurements remain at a bounded distance from the sums
\{\sum_{k=0}^{n}\phi\circ g_{k}\}_{n\geq 1}
. This potential is essentially determined by some variational principle for the measurements. Moreover, as one would expect from reality, the measurements are normally distributed, provided one averages with respect to some well-behaved law.

2.
Geodesic flow on negatively curved spaces and its symbolic representation

In this section, we review some dynamical concepts related to geodesic flows and explain their symbolic structure. We also state analogues of the theorems from the introduction in this more general setting.

2.1.
Geodesic flows

Let
M
be a closed (connected) geodesic space which is negatively curved in the Alexandrov sense. Its universal covering
\widetilde{M}
is therefore a proper
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space and hence contractible. The group
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
acts by isometries on
\widetilde{M}
by deck transformations. Thus, we can (and do) identify
M=\widetilde{M}/\Gamma
. By the Milnor-Švarc theorem,
\Gamma
is finitely generated and quasi-isometric to
\widetilde{M}
, when equipped with any word metric associated to a finite generating set. In particular,
\Gamma
is word hyperbolic. We adopt the following nomenclature, which appears in
[
34
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

M
is referred to as a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space.

The limit set of
\Gamma
on
\widetilde{M}
can be naturally identified with its geometric boundary
\partial\Gamma\cong\partial\widetilde{M}
, and we denote

\mathcal{G}:=\{c:\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{M}:c\text{ is a geodesic},c(-\infty),c(+\infty)\in\partial\Gamma\}.

\mathcal{G}
is equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets; this topology is metrizable. On
\mathcal{G}
there are three natural actions, namely:

(1)

\Gamma
acts cocompactly by isometries with
\gamma\cdot c=\gamma\circ c
;

(2)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts by an involution,
I:c(t)\mapsto c(-t)
;

(3)

\mathbb{R}
acts by
s\cdot c(t):=c(t+s)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{G}/\Gamma
. Since the
\Gamma-
and
\mathbb{R}-
actions commute, there exists an induced
\mathbb{R}
action on
\mathcal{E}
. We denote by
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}\toit
the corresponding flow. Likewise, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action induces an action on
I:\mathcal{E}\toit
, with
I(g_{t})=g_{-t},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.2
.

\mathfrak{g}
is the geodesic flow associated to
\Gamma
.

The above flow was introduced by Gromov
[
35
]
for hyperbolic groups; further details can be found in
[
36
,
37
]
. In the setting of a negatively curved space, the flow is better behaved and can be seen as a direct analogue of the (differentiable) geodesic flow on a hyperbolic manifold. See Bourdon’s article
[
38
]
. We note that the above construction is by no means canonical, and the resulting flow is susceptible to reparametrization. However, Gromov proved that two different geodesic flows associated with
\Gamma
are equivalent in the following sense.

Definition 2.3
.

If
X,Y
are metric spaces equipped with flows
\phi^{X}=(\phi_{t}^{X})_{t}:X\toit,\ \phi^{Y}=(\phi_{t}^{Y})_{t}:Y\toit
we say that a homeomorphism
h:X\to Y
is an equivalence between the flows if it sends orbits of
\phi^{X}
onto orbits of
\phi^{Y}
. In this case the flows are said to be equivalent.

By the above, we will refer to any equivalent flow to
\mathfrak{g}
as a geodesic flow.

Remark 2.4
.

For
M
hyperbolic manifold the space
\mathcal{E}
is homeomorphic to its unit tangent bundle, and
\mathfrak{g}
is flow equivalent to any geodesic flow on
T^{1}M
defined by a smooth Riemannian metric.

In
[
34
]
, the authors show that for locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
spaces one can find a geodesic flow associated to
\Gamma
that is also a topologically mixing Smale flow (also called a metric Anosov flow). For our purposes it will suffice to note three dynamical consequences: expansitivity, shadowing and symbolic representation.

Theorem 2.5

(Expansivity)
.

There exists a geodesic flow
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
and a constant
C_{\mathrm{exp}}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{t}(x),g_{t}(y))\leq C_{\mathrm{exp}}\Rightarrow x=g_{t_{0}}(y)\quad|t_{0}|<C_{\mathrm{exp}}.

Notation
.

A geodesic
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{E}
is periodic if there exists
T>0
so that
\alpha(t+T)=\alpha(t),\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
. The smallest of such
T
is called the period of
\alpha
and is denoted by
\per(\alpha)
.

Theorem 2.6

(Shadowing)
.

The geodesic flow given in the previous theorem also satisfies: given
\delta>0
there exists
C_{\mathrm{sha}}=C_{\mathrm{sha}}(\delta)>0
so that if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are periodic orbits there exists a periodic orbit
\alpha_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\per(\alpha_{3})\leq\per(\alpha_{1})+\per(\alpha_{2})+2C_{\mathrm{sha}}

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\per(\alpha_{1})]}\{\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(\alpha_{1}(t),\alpha_{3}(t))\}<\delta,\sup_{t\in[0,\per(\alpha_{2})]}\{\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(\alpha_{2}(t),\alpha_{3}(t+C_{\mathrm{sha}}))\}<\delta.

The proof of both theorems for Smale flows is the same as for smooth hyperbolic flows, which is standard. See for example Chapter
18
in
[
39
]
. From now on we work with a parametrization
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying both
Theorems

2.5
and
2.6
. Symbolic representation is discussed below.

Notation
.

We fix
\delta_{0}>0
and write
C_{\mathrm{sha}}
for the corresponding constant. If
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are periodic orbits then we write
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2}
for any periodic orbit
\alpha_{3}
satisfying the conclusion of the previous theorem.

2.2.
Weak Bowen Functions and their Livšic cohomology

Recall that
{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
denotes the invariant (probability) measures for the flow
\mathfrak{g}
. Given
x\in\mathcal{E}
and
T>0
we denote

(2.1)

\displaystyle S_{T}\phi(x):=\int_{0}^{T}\phi(g_{t}x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

Fix
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{C_{\mathrm{exp}}}{4}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
be an invariant probability measure of full support. We say that
\phi\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
is a
\mu-
weak Bowen function if there exist
C>0
so that

(\mu\text{-a.e.}\ x,y\in\mathcal{E},\forall T>0):\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq\varepsilon_{0}\Rightarrow\left\lvert S_{T}\phi(x)-S_{T}\phi(y)\right\rvert\leq C.

The Bowen constant of
\phi
is
\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\inf C
, and its Bowen norm is
\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\phi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
. The space of
\mu-
weak Bowen functions is denoted by
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
.

Let us discuss this norm in more detail.

Notation
.

If
x,y\in\mathcal{E},T>0
we write

\distop_{\mathcal{E},T}(x,y)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)

and

B(x,\varepsilon_{0},T)=\{y:\distop_{\mathcal{E},T}(x,y)\leq\varepsilon_{0}\};

these are the
T-
Bowen distance and the
(\varepsilon_{0},T)
-Bowen ball, respectively.

Thus,

\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\phi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}}\sup_{x,y\in B(x,\varepsilon_{0},n)}\lvert S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)\rvert

where in the last supremum it is understood that
x,y
are chosen almost everywhere with respect to
\mu
. Note that this is clearly a norm, and if
(\phi_{k})_{k}\subset\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
verifies
\lim_{k}\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\phi_{k}-\phi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=0
for some function
\phi
, then
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
.

Proposition 2.8
.

\left(\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E}),\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\cdot\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}\right)
is a Banach space.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
let
U_{n}=\{(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}:y\in B(x,\varepsilon_{0},n)\}
. Note that for
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
, denoting
h_{n}(x,y)=S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)
, we have
\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\sup_{n>0}\esssup(h_{n}|U_{n})
, where the essential supremum is taken with respect to
\mu\otimes\mu|U_{n}
.

Let

Z=\mathcal{E}\times\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{n>0}U_{n}\times\{n\}

be the disjoint union of the different
U_{n}
and
\mathcal{E}
. For a given function
u
on
Z
we write
u(\cdot,0),u(\cdot,\cdot,n)
for its restrictions to
\mathcal{E}\times\{0\},U_{n}\times\{n\}
, respectively. Let
\nu
be the (
\sigma-
finite) measure on
Z
induced by
\mu
.

Consider the Banach space
A
, whose underlying space is
L^{\infty}\!\left(Z,\nu\right)
, but with norm

\lVert u\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!A}\coloneqq\lVert u|_{\mathcal{E}\times\{0\}}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\sup_{n>0}\lVert u|_{U_{n}\times\{n\}}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}.

Notice that this norm is equivalent to the standard
L^{\infty}
norm on
Z
.

We have that the functions with the Bowen property embed isometrically into
A
by
\iota_{\phi}(a)=\phi(x)
for
a=(x,0)\in\mathcal{E}\times\{0\}
and
\iota_{\phi}(a)=S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)
if
a=(x,y,n)\in U_{n}\times\{n\}
.

It is thus enough to show that
\iota(\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E}))\subset A
is closed. Let
(\phi_{k})_{k}\subset\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
and assume that
u_{k}\coloneqq\iota_{\phi_{k}}\xrightarrow[k\mapsto\infty]{}u
; we want to show that there is a function
\phi:D_{\phi}\subset\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
so that
u(x,y,n)=S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)
whenever
\distop_{\mathcal{E},n}(x,y)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Note that
\phi(x)
can only be defined as
\phi(x)=\lim_{k}u_{k}(x,0)
, which exists
\mu-
almost everywhere.

On the other hand, we know that for every given
n
and
k
,
S_{n}\phi_{k}(x)-S_{n}\phi_{k}(y)=u_{k}(x,y,n)\xrightarrow[k\mapsto\infty]{}u(x,y,n)
. Also, for
\mu\text{-a.e.}
it holds for every
n
,
S_{n}\phi_{k}(x)\xrightarrow[k\mapsto\infty]{}S_{n}\phi(x)
(pointwise convergence together with the bounded convergence theorem). Hence

u_{k}(x,y,n)=S_{n}\phi_{k}(x)-S_{n}\phi_{k}(y)\xrightarrow[k\mapsto\infty]{}S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)

and so

S_{n}\phi(x)-S_{n}\phi(y)=u(x,y,n)

for every
n
, for almost every
x,y
with
\distop_{\mathcal{E},n}(x,y)\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, which means that
u=\iota_{\phi}
, thus the image is closed.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

We say that
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
is a Livšic coboundary if there exists
u\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mathcal{E},\mu\right)
which is differentiable along orbits of
\mathfrak{g}
and satisfies
\phi=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\circ g_{t}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}|_{t=0}
. The space of Livšic coboundaries is denoted by
\mathrm{Cob}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
.

Two functions
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
are said to be Livšic cohomologous (
\phi\sim\psi
) if their difference is a Livšic coboundary.

Note that
\mathrm{Cob}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})\subset\left(\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E}),\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\cdot\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}\right)
is a closed subspace, thus

\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})/\thicksim\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})}{\mathrm{Cob}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})}

is a Banach space with respect to the norm

(2.2)

\displaystyle\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert[\phi]\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}=\inf\{\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\psi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}:\psi\sim\phi\}.

Definition 2.10
.

We say that
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
is antisymmetric if
\phi\circ I\sim-\phi
. We denote by
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
the subspace of
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
consisting of antisymmetric elements.

With these definitions we have the following generalization of
Theorems

A
and
B
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
M
be a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space, and let
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
be fully supported. Denote by
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
.

(1)

There exists a Banach isomorphism

\Psi:\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma),\lVert\cdot\rVert\right)\to\left(\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E}),\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\cdot\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert\right).

(2)

For each
[\phi]\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})/\thicksim
there exists a measure
\mu_{[\phi]}=\mu_{\phi}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
which can be characterized as the unique element of this space satisfying

h_{\mu_{\phi}}\!\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)+\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\phi}=\sup_{\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(X)}\left(h_{\mu}\!\left(\mathfrak{g}\right)+\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\right).

For every
t\neq 0
, the stochastic process defined by
(\phi\circ g_{nt})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
is measure-theoretically isomorphic to a Bernoulli process with finitely many symbols.

An important part of this article is devoted to extending the classical cohomology theory of Livšic
[
17
]
for non-regular weak Bowen functions. Consider
\phi:D_{\phi}\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
: with no loss of generality we assume that
D_{\phi}
is
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant, and also dense, since
\mu
is fully supported. Let
x\in\mathcal{E}
be contained in a periodic orbit
\alpha
, and suppose that
y\in D_{\phi}
is so that

\sup_{t\in[0,\per(\alpha)]}\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq C_{\mathrm{exp}}.

Then the limit

\lim_{T\to\+\infty}\frac{S_{T}\phi(y)}{T}

exists, does not depend on the point
y
chosen with the above rule, and in fact only depends on
\alpha
. These facts are simple to prove: existence follows almost directly from subadditivity of the sequence
(S_{n}\phi(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, while independence of the chosen point (
y
and
x\in\alpha
) is consequence of the weak Bowen property. See
Section

5
where an analogous fact is proven.

Definition 2.12
.

The average of
\phi
on the periodic orbit
\alpha
is

\av_{\alpha}(\phi)\coloneqq\lim_{T\to+\infty}\frac{S_{T}\phi(y)}{T},

where
x\in\alpha
and
y\in D_{\phi}
satisfy

\sup_{t\in[0,\per(\alpha)]}\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{t}x,g_{t}y)\leq C_{\mathrm{exp}}.

This definition is natural, since if
D(\phi)=\mathcal{E}
, then
\av_{\alpha}(\phi)
is just the integral of
\phi
with respect to the homogeneous probability measure supported on
\alpha
. We will prove:

Theorem E
.

Let
\mu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
be fully supported. Then
\phi,\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
are Livšic cohomologous if and only if for every periodic orbit
\alpha
of
\mathfrak{g}
it holds
\av_{\alpha}(\phi)=\av_{\alpha}(\psi)
.

This immediately implies the claim stated in the introduction that
\Ker c_{2}
is infinite-dimensional
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
]
.

Corollary 2.13
.

For
M,\mu
as in
Theorem

2.11
, the kernel
\Ker\left(c_{2}:H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(M,\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(M;\mathbb{R})\right)
is infinite dimensional.

Take
(\alpha_{n})_{n}
an infinite sequence of different (oriented) periodic orbits for the flow
\mathfrak{g}
, and choose a pairwise disjoint open family
(U_{n})_{n}
so that
U_{n}=I(U_{n})
is a neighborhood of
\alpha_{n}
in
\mathcal{E}
. Standard methods allow one to construct for each
n
a Lipschitz function
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
so that
\phi_{n}|_{\alpha_{n}}>0
and
\phi_{n}|_{U_{n}^{c}}=0
. Define
\phi_{n}\circ I=-\phi_{n}
.

Theorem

E
directly implies that
\{[\phi_{n}]\}_{n}\subset\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})/\thicksim
is linearly independent, therefore the target space is infinite dimensional, which implies the same for
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma),\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
, by
Theorem

2.11
. Since
\Ker c_{2}
is isomorphic to
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)/\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
, the claim follows.
∎

There exist analogous versions of the Limit Theorems and their corresponding Corollaries for the general setting discussed in
Theorem

2.11
. The proofs will be given in this framework.

2.3.
Symbolic Representation of the Geodesic flow

A key fact in the differentiable setting is that the geodesic flow of a negatively curved metric is an Anosov flow, and in particular it admits Markov partitions
[
40
,
41
]
. This allows one to use the powerful machinery of symbolic dynamics to study the flow. In a recent work of Constantine, Lafont and Thompson
[
34
]
the same type of structure is established for compact locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
spaces, refining a previous construction due to Pollicott
[
42
]
. We explain this below.

Given
{A}
a finite set (the alphabet) and
R:{A}\times{A}\to\{0,1\}
consider

\widetilde{\Sigma}=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{R}=\left\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}:x_{n}\in{A},R(x_{n},x_{n+1})=1,\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

By identifying
{A}=\{1,\cdots,d\}
we get that
R=(R_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\in\Mat_{d}(\{0,1\})
. From now on we assume that
R
is irreducible and aperiodic, and in particular, there exists
M>0
such that for all
k\geq M
,
R^{k}
is a positive matrix. If
M=1
(
R_{ij}=1
for all
i,j
) then
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is called the full shift.

Definition 2.14
.

By a subshift of finite type (SFT) we mean a space
\Sigma_{R}
as described above (in particular,
R
is irreducible and aperiodic). The number
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
R^{M}
is positive will be referred to as the specification constant of the subshift.

The space
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is topologized as a subset of
{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, where the latter is given the product topology induced by the discrete one on
{A}
. It follows that
\widetilde{\Sigma}\subset{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is closed, and homeomorphic to a Cantor space (Moore-Kline theorem). The metric on
{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
defined as

\distop_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle y}}{\underline{\textstyle y}}{\underline{\scriptstyle y}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle y}})=\frac{1}{2^{N(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle y}}{\underline{\textstyle y}}{\underline{\scriptstyle y}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle y}})+1}},\quad N=\max\{n:x_{i}=y_{i},i=-N,\ldots N\}

is compatible with the product topology, therefore
{A}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\widetilde{\Sigma}
are metrizable. In the rest of the article it is assumed that these spaces are equipped the above metric.

The shift map on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is the (bi-Lipschitz) homeomorphism
\tau:\widetilde{\Sigma}\toit
,
\tau(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=(x_{n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Fix a SFT
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, and let
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a continuous bounded function. Here and in other parts of this article we write

(2.3)

\displaystyle S_{n}^{\tau}f=\begin{cases}0&n=0\\
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}f\circ\tau^{k}&n>0\\
\sum_{k=n}^{-1}f\circ\tau^{k}&n<0\end{cases}

(Birkhoff’s sums of
f
associated to the dynamics
\tau
): then
(S_{n}^{\tau}f)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an additive cocycle over
\mathbb{Z}
. On
\widetilde{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
consider the
\mathbb{Z}-
action given by
n\cdot(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t)=(\tau^{n}\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t-S_{n}^{\tau}f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}))
, and denote by
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}=\widetilde{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. The translation flow on
\widetilde{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{R}
induces a continuous flow
(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}
by

\displaystyle\tau_{t}([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s])=[\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s+t].

Definition 2.15
.

The flow
\mathfrak{t}=(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\toit
is the suspension flow of
\tau
under the function
f
.

Recall that an invariant probability for a flow is ergodic if every (Borel) set that is invariant under flow has zero or full measure.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
M
be a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space. Then there exist a reparametrization
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t}:\mathcal{E}\toit)_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of the associated geodesic flow, a SFT
\widetilde{\Sigma}
, a Hölder function
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and a Hölder map
h:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\to\mathcal{E}
satisfying:

(1)

h
is surjective, and
h\circ\tau_{t}=g_{t}\circ h
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
;

(2)

there exists a closed nowhere dense set
B\subset\mathcal{E}
so that
h
is one-to-one on
h^{-1}(\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}g_{t}(B))
;

(3)

there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
so that
h
is at most
k
-to-one;

(4)

h
sends periodic orbits of
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}
to periodic orbits of
\mathfrak{g}
;

(5)

if
\nu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
has full support and is ergodic, then there exists a unique
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})
so that
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\circ h^{-1}=\nu
.

This is classical for hyperbolic flows
[
41
]
. In the previous generality, everything is proven in
[
34
]
, except for the last point, that follows from the others.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

Let
\nu\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{E})
be an ergodic measure of full support and
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
as in the previous theorem. Then
h
induces Banach isomorphisms
h_{*}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
,
h_{*}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})/\thicksim
.

The above theorem allows us to reduce the study of some dynamical properties of
\mathfrak{g}
to the corresponding ones in
\mathfrak{t}
, which typically are more manageable. As an illustration, if
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
, then there exists some
\tau
-invariant measure
\nu_{\tau}
such that

(2.4)

\displaystyle H\in\mathcal{C}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})\Rightarrow\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}(H)=\frac{\int_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}\ \left(\int_{0}^{f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}H([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\tau}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}{\int f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu_{\tau}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})},

and conversely, for
\nu_{\tau}\in{P}_{\tau}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
the above formula defines an invariant measure for
\mathfrak{t}
. It follows that there exists a one-to-one correspondence between invariant measures on
{P}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
and
{P}_{\tau}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
. Moreover,
\nu
is ergodic if and only if
\nu_{\tau}
is ergodic (the definition of ergodicity for
\tau
is analogous to the case of flows). See for example
[
43
]
, Chapter
6
.

Corollary 2.18
.

In the same hypotheses as in the previous theorem, there exists a one-to-one correspondence between fully supported ergodic probability measures for
\mathfrak{g}
, and fully supported ergodic measures for
\tau
.

After discussing some of the structure of SFTs we will improve
Corollary

2.17
, establishing that
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathcal{E})
is in fact isomorphic to the space
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\tau}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})
.

Other possible extensions

To apply our arguments we rely on the existence of the symbolic model for the flow, but once one reaches this setting the rest of the theory goes through. In particular, the same applies to Axiom A flows, which is the case of the geodesic flow restricted to its non-wandering set, when
M
is a convex compact
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space: this fact is also proven in
[
34
]
. We also mention that the content of this article gives a coarse version of thermodynamic formalism for classical hyperbolic systems, as Anosov diffeomorphisms and flows.

The idea of studying equilibrium states for the Gromov-Mineyev flow of a locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space also appears in the recent work of Dilsavor and Thompson
[
44
]
(based on the first author Ph.D. thesis
[
45
]
). The focus there is on constructing equilibrium states for some families of continuous sub-additive potentials (in our terminology, continuous quasicocycles for the flow), while the arguments are more dependent on the geometry of the group, e.g. constructing some Patterson-Sullivan type density and using it to assemble the equilibrium state. In the compact case, the results here give another proof of existence and uniqueness of equilibrium states for the families that they consider, but their geometrical approach allows them to consider non-compact cases as well. It seems likely that further synergy between their results and ours can be exploited.

3.
Quasimorphisms on hyperbolic groups

Given a finitely generated group
\Gamma
, one can consider bounded real-valued cochains and proceed analogously to the case of topological spaces in order to define its bounded cohomology. It is a result of Gromov (page 257 of
[
2
]
) that if
M
is a
\mathrm{K}(\Gamma,1)
, the bounded cohomology of
M
and
\Gamma
are naturally isomorphic. This is useful because, as mentioned in the introduction, to study the
\Ker c_{2}
one can study instead the space
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
. We remind the reader that, by the Cartan–Hadamard theorem, if
M
is a locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space then it is a
\mathrm{K}(\pi_{1}(M,*),1)
,

Notation
.

if
X
is a set we denote
\ell^{\infty}(X)=\{f:X\to\mathbb{R}:(\exists C>0)\ \forall x\in X,\ |f(x)|\leq C\}
, and we write
\ell^{\infty}=\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{N})
. The
\ell^{\infty}
norm on
\ell^{\infty}(X)
is defined as usual.

Recall that
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
are said to be cohomologous if
L-L^{\prime}\in\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)
. Note that
L\mapsto\lVert\delta L\rVert
is a seminorm on
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, hence it induces naturally a norm on
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
. However, this norm is not complete, and it is more convenient to use a different one. Fix a finite generating set
S
of
\Gamma
, and denote
S_{e}=S\cup\{1_{\Gamma}\}
. For
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
and
a,b\in\Gamma
we are writing

\delta L(a,b)=L(ab)-L(a)-L(b).

Define

(3.1)

\displaystyle\lVert L\rVert=\lVert L\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=\lVert L|_{S_{e}}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}+\lVert\delta L\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}.

Proposition 3.1
.

(\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma),\lVert\cdot\rVert)
is a Banach space. Moreover, if
S^{\prime}
is another finite generating set of
\Gamma
then the norms
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S^{\prime}}
are equivalent.

Clearly
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is a semi-norm, and if
\lVert L\rVert=0
then, on one hand
\delta L=0
and hence
L
is a homomorphism, and on the other hand it vanishes on a generating set, so it is the
0
homomorphism. Thus
\lVert\cdot\rVert
is norm. The equivalence of the norms for finite
S,S^{\prime}
is obvious, so it remains to show completeness. We proceed similarly as in
Proposition

2.8
.

Consider the space
\mathcal{S}=\ell^{\infty}(S_{e})\times\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma\times\Gamma)
with the norm

\lVert(l,u)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=\lVert l\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}(S_{e})}+\lVert u\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma\times\Gamma)}.

\mathcal{S}
is a Banach space, and
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
embeds isometrically in it as
L\to(L|_{S_{e}},\delta L)
. We claim that the image of this map is closed. Let
(l_{n},u_{n})_{n}=(L_{n}|_{S_{e}},\delta L_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathcal{S}
and assume that
l_{n}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}l
and
u_{n}=\delta L_{n}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}u
, thus in particular
\lVert(l,u)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}<\infty
: we want to show that
l=L|S
and
\delta L=u
for some
L:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
(which necessary will satisfy
\lVert\delta L\rVert<\infty
, hence
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
). Let us show inductively that
L_{n}
converges pointwise in
B_{k}=\{w\in\Gamma:\lvert w\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}\leq k\}
.

For
k=0
and
k=1
this is true since
L_{n}|_{S_{e}}=l_{n}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}l
. Assume it is convergent for
k
, take
x\in B_{k+1}
and consider
s\in S,\gamma\in S^{k}
, so that
x=s\gamma
. It follows that

L_{n}(x)=L_{n}(s\gamma)=u_{n}(s,\gamma)+l_{n}(s)+L_{n}|_{B_{k}}(\gamma).

It is no loss of generality to suppose
\lvert x\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=k+1
, otherwise we are already in the induction hypotheses. Note also that
x=s_{1}\gamma_{1}
, then
if
x=s_{1}\gamma_{1}
, then

u_{n}(s,\gamma)+l_{n}(s)+L_{n}|_{B_{k}}(\gamma)=L_{n}(x)=u_{n}(s_{1},\gamma_{1})+l_{n}(s_{1})+L_{n}|_{B_{k}}(\gamma)(\gamma_{1}),

Since
u_{n},l_{n}
and
L_{n}|_{B_{k}}
are convergent, we get that
L_{n}(x)
is Cauchy, hence convergent, so
L_{n}|_{B_{k+1}}
converges pointwise to a map that we denote
L|_{B_{k+1}}
.
This way we have
L
defined on
\bigcup_{k\geq 0}B_{k}=\Gamma
. From the pointwise convergence we also get that if
a\in B_{k},b\in B_{k^{\prime}}
(
\Rightarrow ab\in B_{k+k^{\prime}}
)

\displaystyle\delta L_{n}(a,b)=u_{n}(a,b)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}u(a,b)

\displaystyle\delta L_{n}(a,b)=L_{n}(ab)-L_{n}(a)-L_{n}(b)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}L(ab)-L(a)-L(b)=\delta L(a,b).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

In the proof above, note that
\delta L(a,a^{-1})=L(1_{\Gamma})-L(a)-L(a^{-1})
, hence
\delta L
and
L|_{S_{e}}
determine
L
on
S^{-1}
. We deduce that we can assume that
S
is symmetric in what follows.

In other geometrical problems it would be important to consider distances in the space of quasimorphisms that do not possess this feature.

Since
\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)\subset\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is closed with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}
, from the above it follows directly that
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is a Banach space with respect to the quotient norm

(3.2)

\displaystyle\lVert[L]\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}=\inf\{\lVert L^{\prime}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}:L^{\prime}\sim L\}.

Moreover, the induced norm on
\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})\oplus\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\approx\frac{\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})}
is given by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\lVert[L]\rVert=\inf\{\lVert\delta L^{\prime}\rVert:L^{\prime}\sim L\},

which in particular does not depend on
S
.

We collect the relation between quasimorphism and bounded cohomology in the following.

Proposition 3.3
.

If
\Gamma
is finitely generated then there exists a short exact sequence of linear maps

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\rightarrow H^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{c_{2}}H^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})\rightarrow 0.

Moreover, there exists a linear isometry

\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})\oplus\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\approx\Ker c_{2}.

The existence of the short exact sequence is well known, and can be found for example in
[
46
]
: the linear map
\Gamma:\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\Ker c_{2}
given by
\Gamma(L)=[\delta L]

is surjective and vanishes precisely on
\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})\oplus\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)
. On the other hand, the norm on
\Ker c_{2}
is of the form

\lVert[B]\rVert=\inf\{\lVert B+\delta b\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}:b\in\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)\}

(see
[
5
]
), thus

\lVert\Gamma([L])\rVert=\inf\{\lVert\delta(L+b)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}:b\in\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)\}=\inf\{\lVert\delta(L^{\prime})\rVert:L^{\prime}\sim L\}=\lVert[L]\rVert.

∎

Due to the above, we will focus on the space of unbounded quasimorphisms
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)=\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
. Each cohomology class of quasimorphism has a well behaved representative, namely a (unique) quasimorphism
\overline{L}
so that for every
g\in\Gamma,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\overline{L}(g^{n})=nL(g)
. From here it follows that

\overline{L}(g):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{L(g^{n})}{n}

See
[
46
]
, or
Lemma

4.14
where an analogous statement is discussed.

Definition 3.4
.

L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is homogeneous if for every
g\in\Gamma,n\in\mathbb{Z}
it holds
L(g^{n})=nL(g)
. The set of homogeneous quasimorphisms on
\Gamma
is denoted by
\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
.

Corollary 3.5
.

If
L\in\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, then it is constant on conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
.

Indeed,
|L(h\cdot gh^{-1})-L(g)|\leq 2\lVert\delta L\rVert
for every
g,h
. It follows that for
h
fixed the map
L^{\prime}(g)=L(h\cdot g\cdot h^{-1})-L(g)
is a bounded homogeneous quasimorphism, hence identically zero.
∎

Consider the inclusion
\iota:\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, This is a continuous map and has a left inverse
p(L)=\overline{L}
. Note that
|p(L)(g)-L(g)|\leq\lVert\delta L\rVert
for every
g
and so
\lVert p(L)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}\leq\lVert L\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}
, thus
p:\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is continuous.

Since any bounded homogeneous quasimorphisms is necessarily trivial, we obtain:

Corollary 3.6
.

The spaces
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
and
\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
are Banach isomorphic when equipped with the norm associated to some finite symmetric generating set
S\subset\Gamma
.

Assumption:
From now on we work with homogeneous quasimorphisms.

3.1.
quasimorphisms in negative curvature

Now let
M
be a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space, and let
\mathfrak{g}:\mathcal{E}\toit
be a geodesic flow associated to
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
, as in
Section

2.1
. In this part we will show that elements of
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
can be uniquely represented by some equivalence classes of functions defined on the set of periodic orbits of
\mathfrak{g}
. We introduce the notation

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}):=\{\alpha:\text{ oriented closed orbit of $\mathfrak{g}$}\}

and for
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
, we denote its minimal period by
\per(\alpha)
. We recall that for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
we write
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2}
if
\alpha_{3}
verifies the conclusion of
Theorem

2.6
. From now on we choose
\delta
sufficiently small so that if
c,d
are curves in
M
whose Hausdorff distance is less than
2\delta
, then they are homotopic. By compactness of
M
we can also guarantee the existence of some
E>0
so that if
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2},\alpha_{4}=\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2}
then

(3.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\per(\alpha_{3})=\per(\alpha_{4})]}\{\distop_{\mathcal{E}}(\alpha_{3}(t),\alpha_{4}(t))\}<E.

We fix
S
a finite symmetric set of generators for
\Gamma
as before. By word-hyperbolicity of
\Gamma
, any
g\in\Gamma
is conjugate to some element that can be written in a reduced cyclic word on
S
, and if
g_{1},g_{2}
are two such elements, then
g_{1}
is conjugate to some cyclic permutation on the letters of
g_{2}
. See Chapter 4 in
[
47
]
. For
g\in\Gamma
its translation length is defined as

\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\distop_{\Gamma}(g^{n},1)}{n}.

It is well known that this is a class function; moreover,
\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)=\distop_{\Gamma}(\widetilde{g},1)
, where
\widetilde{g}
is conjugate to
g
and can be written as a reduced cyclic word on
S
of minimal size. Word-hyperbolicity of
\Gamma
also implies that any of its elements
g
fixes a unique geodesic
c_{g}:\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{M}
(called the axis of
g
), and for
x\in\mathrm{Im}(c_{g})
it holds that
|\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)-\distop_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{g}\cdot x,x)|<C
, where
\widetilde{g}
is as in the previous line and
C
does not depend on
g,x
. For
\phi:\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{R}
define

\displaystyle|\delta\phi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})|=\inf_{\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2}}\left\{|\phi(\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2})-\phi(\alpha_{1})-\phi(\alpha_{2})|\right\},

\displaystyle\lVert\delta\phi\rVert:=\sup_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\{|\delta\phi(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})|\},

and let

\displaystyle\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})):=\{\phi:\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathbb{R}:\lVert\delta\phi\rVert<\infty\}.

Definition 3.7
.

Two functions
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
are cohomologous if their difference is uniformly bounded. We denote

(3.6)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))}{\ell^{\infty}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))}.

To define a norm on
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
so that (the finite set)
V=V_{N}=\{\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}):\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)\leq N\}
satisfies

\mathcal{E}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in V}\bigcup_{x\in\alpha}B(x,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},\per(\alpha)):

we say that
V
is generating. Let

\lVert\phi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!V}=\lVert\phi|_{V}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}+\lVert\delta\phi\rVert.

Proceeding as in the proof of
Propositions

2.8
and
3.1
one verifies that this is a Banach norm, and for different
N^{\prime},V_{N^{\prime}}
the corresponding norms are equivalent. Moreover, with

\lVert[\phi]\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!V}\coloneqq\inf\{\lVert\phi^{\prime}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!V}:\phi\sim\phi^{\prime}\}

the space
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
inherits the Banach structure. Denote by

\mathrm{QM}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))

the (closed) subspace of
\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
consisting of functions
\phi
that are cohomologous to
-\phi\circ I
, and by

\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))

the corresponding quotient.

The relevance of periodic orbits comes from the following observation. Since homogeneous quasimorphisms are constant on conjugacy classes, and conjugacy classes correspond to closed geodesics in
M
, it is natural to interpret a quasimorphism as a function on the periodic orbits of the geodesic flow. The quasimorphism relation

|L(gh)-L(g)-L(h)|\leq C

translates into the following: although periodic orbits cannot be concatenated, the shadowing property produces a periodic orbit that successively follows two given ones, and the value assigned to this shadowing orbit differs from the sum of the individual values by a uniformly bounded error.

Theorem 3.8
.

There exists a bijection
B:\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
and constants
b_{1},b_{2}
so that

\forall g\in\Gamma,\ b_{1}\cdot\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)-b_{2}\leq\per(B([g]))\leq b_{1}\cdot\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)+b_{2}.

If
S\subset\Gamma,V\subset\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
are corresponding generating sets, then this bijection induces a Banach isomorphism

B_{*}:\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}\right)\to\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!V}\right).

We divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1: correspondence between conjugacy classes and periodic orbits.

Since
M
is negatively curved, every free homotopy class of loops in
M
contains a unique closed geodesic. Due to compactness of
M
, every
g\in\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
is represented by a curve of the form
h_{g}\ast c_{g}\ast h_{g}^{-1}
, where
c_{g}
is a closed (parametrized) geodesic, and
h_{g}
is a geodesic segment of length bounded by
a
, where
a>0
does not depend on
g
. This defines a bijection between
\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)
and the set of closed geodesics in
M
,
[g]\mapsto c_{g}
.

If
\widetilde{c}_{g}:\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{M}
is a lift of
c_{g}
, then
\widetilde{c}_{g}
determines a periodic orbit of the geodesic flow
\mathfrak{g}
. Denote this orbit by
\alpha_{g}
. Altogether, this defines a bijection

B:\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}),\quad B([g])=\alpha_{g}.

Step 2: comparison of periods and translation lengths.

Let
g\in\Gamma
and let
c_{g}
be the closed geodesic corresponding to
[g]
. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is a reparametrization of the Gromov geodesic flow, the period of
\alpha_{g}
is uniformly comparable to the length of
c_{g}
. Using the relation between the length of
c_{g}
and the translation length
\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)
, we obtain constants
b_{1},b_{2}>0
such that

b_{1}\,\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)-b_{2}\leq\per(B([g]))\leq b_{1}\,\tlength_{\Gamma}(g)+b_{2}.

Step 3: correspondence of quasimorphisms.

Let
L\in\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
and define, for a closed geodesic
\alpha
,

B_{*}(L)(\alpha)=L(B^{-1}(\alpha)).

Since homogeneous quasimorphisms are constant on conjugacy classes (see
Corollary

3.5
), this definition is well posed.

Consider
g_{i}\in\Gamma,c_{i}=c_{g_{i}},\alpha_{i}=B(g_{i}),i=1,2
and let
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2}
: recall that (
Theorem

2.6
) this denotes a periodic orbit of
\mathfrak{g}
that remains at short distance of
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
, with bounded transitions between them. It follows that the corresponding parametrized geodesic
c_{3}
is (freely) homotopic to a closed curve of the form

\gamma=u\ast c_{1}\ast v\ast c_{2}\ast w

where the length of
u,v
and
w
are uniformly bounded, independently of
g_{1},g_{2}
. Choose any
g_{3}
in the conjugacy class of
\gamma
and note that
B(g_{3})=\alpha_{3}
: in particular the translation length of
g_{3}
is uniformly comparable with

\per(c_{3})\approx\per(\alpha_{3})\approx\per(\alpha_{1})+\per(\alpha_{2})\approx\tlength(g_{1})+\tlength(g_{2}).

Moreover,
|L(g_{3})-L(g_{1})-L(g_{2})|
is uniformly bounded by a multiple of
\lVert L\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!S}
, hence the same is true for
|B_{*}(L)(\alpha_{1}\star\alpha_{2})-B_{*}(L)(\alpha_{1})-B_{*}(L)(\alpha_{2})|
. Note also that by construction,

(3.7)

B_{*}(L)(I(\alpha))=-B_{*}(L)(\alpha)

Recall that
\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is Banach isomorphic to
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
(
Corollary

3.6
). We thus have constructed a continuous linear map

B_{*}:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\approx\mathrm{HQM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
, which is easily seen to be injective: if
L-L^{\prime}\in\ell^{\infty}(\Gamma)
then

B_{*}(L)-B_{*}(L^{\prime})\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
.

Let us prove that it is also surjective. Take
K\in\mathrm{QM}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
and define
L:\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{R}
by

g,h\in\Gamma\Rightarrow L(hgh^{-1})\coloneqq K(\alpha_{g}).

We consider
R>C_{\mathrm{sha}}
so that any two pair of points in
M
can be joined by a broken geodesic of size
R
, and say that
g\in\Gamma
is small if
there exists a closed geodesic
c
and paths
u,v,w
of size
\leq R
so that
g\approx ucvc^{-1}w
. The fact that
K
is bounded and antisymmetric implies that
L
is bounded on small words. Now suppose that we are given
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Gamma
, that we write in terms of the set of generators
S
,

\displaystyle g_{1}=s_{1}\cdots s_{k}

\displaystyle g_{2}=r_{1}\cdots r_{l}.

Let
g_{3}=g_{1}\ast g_{2}
with corresponding closed geodesic
c_{3}
, and consider the closed curve
\gamma=u\ast c_{1}\ast v\ast c_{2}\ast w
as above. If
s_{k}\neq r_{1}^{-1}
then
\gamma
and the closed geodesic corresponding to
g_{3}
differ by (a uniformly bounded amount of) small words, hence

L(g_{3})\approx K(c_{3})\approx K(c_{1})+K(c_{2}).

In general,
g_{1}\ast g_{2}=s_{1}\cdots s_{i}r_{j}\cdots r_{l}
with
s_{i}\neq r_{j}^{-1}
and by the previous part and antisymmetry of
K
,

\displaystyle L(g_{3})

\displaystyle=L(s_{1}\cdots s_{i})+L(r_{j}\cdots r_{l})\approx L(s_{1}\cdots s_{i}s_{i+1}\cdots s_{k})+L((s_{i}\cdots s_{k})^{-1}r_{i+1}\cdots r_{l})

\displaystyle=L(g_{1})+L(g_{2}).

We thus get that
L
defines an homogeneous quasimorphism and
B_{*}L=K
.

By the open mapping theorem (or just by following the constants along in the construction of
L
above) one gets that
B
is a Banach isomorphism.
∎

At this point the natural idea would be to use
Theorem

2.16
and try to pass from
\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
to an analogous space defined for the suspension flow
\mathfrak{t}
. Since the periodic orbits of
\mathfrak{t}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the periodic points of the shift
\tau:\Sigma\toit
, we could reduce even further and work at the symbolic level. Unfortunately, this approach has a significative drawback. The semi-conjugacy between
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{g}
is one-to-one only for periodic points in an open and dense subset of
\mathcal{E}
, hence a given
\phi\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
could be lifted only on a subset of its domain, and indeed this set of problematic periodic points is typically exponentially large. This would make it very difficult to achieve a lift that preserves the “quasimorphism” property on periodic points of
\mathfrak{\tau}
, which is central to what we are doing. Instead, we work at a coarser level and show that a certain cohomology class of
\phi
is well defined even with this partially available information. To achieve this we now study the corresponding concept of quasimorphism on a SFT.

4.
