is a Weak Bernoulli process, then it is isomorphic to a Bernoulli process, meaning a system having a finite independent generator.

Back to
(\widetilde{\Sigma},\widetilde{\mu}_{L},\tau)
, note that
\xi=\{[a]:a\in{A}\}
is a generator.

Corollary 7.36
.

The partition
\xi
is Weak Bernoulli. Therefore
(\widetilde{\Sigma},\widetilde{\mu}_{L},\tau)
is isomorphic to a Bernoulli shift.

Indeed,
Corollary

7.31
shows that
(\widetilde{\Sigma},\tau)
is quasi-Bernoulli, and the statement follows combining both theorems cited above.
∎

8.
Proofs of Theorems A and B

In this section we summarize the constructions developed above and prove the main results
Theorems

A
and
B
.
The limit theorems (
Theorems

C
and
D
) and their corollaries are discussed in
Section

9
.
We also establish the auxiliary
Theorem

1.7
.

Let
M
be a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space and consider
\mathfrak{g}=(g_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
the associated geodesic flow, parametrized in such a way that it is a metric Anosov flow. Consider the continuous linear isomorphism
B_{*}:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
given in
Theorem

3.8
. Fix
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\mathrm{erg}}({E})
of full support.

Let
\mathfrak{t}=(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\toit
be a suspension flow as in
Theorem

2.16
with corresponding semi-conjugacy
h
. This is also a metric Anosov flow, and we emphasize that there exists an open and dense subset
{E}_{0}\subset{E}
such that every
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
intersecting
{E}_{0}
lifts to a unique periodic orbit of
\mathfrak{t}
. Note also that there exists a natural involution
I_{f}:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\toit
that interchanges the direction of orbits, and
h\circ I_{f}=I\circ h
.

We now describe the sequence of constructions that associate to a class
[L_{\mathfrak{g}}]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
a Bowen function on
\Sigma
. Given
[L_{\mathfrak{g}}]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
, it induces a function
L_{\mathfrak{t}}:\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(h^{-1}({E}_{0}))\to\mathbb{R}
, with the quasimorphism property

\forall\alpha,\beta\text{ such that }\alpha\star\beta\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(h^{-1}({E}_{0}))\Rightarrow|L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\alpha\star\beta)-L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\alpha)-L_{\mathfrak{t}}(\beta)|\leq\lVert\delta L_{\mathfrak{g}}\rVert.

Observe that if
L_{\mathfrak{g}}\sim L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
, then
\lVert L_{\mathfrak{t}}-L_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
.

Recall that by
Lemma

5.28
there exists a bijection
\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{t})\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\tau)
, and thus there exists a bijection
S:\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})\to\Sigma_{0}\subset\Sigma
, where
\Sigma_{0}
is a dense set of periodic orbits of
\tau
. We use
S
to transfer the quasimorphism structure to
\Sigma_{0}
, and then extend it to a locally constant quasicocycle
\bm{L}=(L^{(n)})_{n}
.

Below, for
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g})
we write

\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha^{n}),\quad\alpha^{n}=\underbrace{\alpha\star\alpha\star\cdots\alpha}_{n\text{ times }}

for the value of the periodic measure supported on
\alpha
, evaluated on
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Similarly for periodic measures on
\Sigma
.

By construction of
\bm{L}
,

\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})\rightarrow\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathrm{m}_{S(\alpha)}(\bm{L})

Lemma 8.1
.

Suppose that
L_{\mathfrak{g}},L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}))
are such that for every
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})
,
\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})
.
Then
L_{\mathfrak{g}}\sim L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
.

Arguing as in
Section

5.1
, it suffices to show that
\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})
for every
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})
. After homogenizing
L_{\mathfrak{g}},L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
(thus
L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha^{n})=nL_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha)
) we can assume that if
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})
then
\mathrm{m}_{\alpha}(L_{\mathfrak{g}})=L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha)
, and similarly for
L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})
. Using densitiy of
{E}_{0}
we can choose
\beta\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})
so that
\alpha\star\beta\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})
. Then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle|L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha^{n})-L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\alpha^{n})|

\displaystyle\leq|L_{\mathfrak{g}}((\alpha\star\beta)^{n})-L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha^{n})-L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\beta^{n})|+|L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}((\alpha\star\beta)^{n})-L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\alpha^{n})-L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\beta^{n})|

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\delta L_{\mathfrak{g}}\rVert+\lVert\delta L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}\rVert

and thus
L_{\mathfrak{g}}(\alpha)=L_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}(\alpha)
.
∎

As a consequence, if
\bm{L},\bm{L}^{\prime}
are two induced quasi-cocyles so that for every
\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in\Sigma_{0}
,
\mathrm{m}_{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}}(\bm{L})=\mathrm{m}_{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}}(\bm{L}^{\prime})
, then
\bm{L}\sim\bm{L}^{\prime}
. This in particular shows that the quasicocycle corresponding to
L_{\mathfrak{g}}
is cohomologous to the one corresponding to
-L_{\mathfrak{g}}\circ I
.

Fix
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}({E})
of full support, and let
\mu\in{P}_{\tau}(\Sigma)
be the corresponding measure. Since
\mu
is fully supported we can apply
Theorem

5.2
and use
\bm{L}
to obtain
\varphi_{L}\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
. Recall that the cohomology class of Bowen function
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
is represented uniquely by the cohomology class of some Bowen function
\hat{\phi}\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma_{f})
(
Corollary

5.29
). By the previous paragraph,
\varphi_{L}
has the following antisymmetry property.

Definition 8.2
.

A Bowen function
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
is antisymmetric if
\hat{\phi}\sim-\hat{\phi}\circ I_{f}
. The set of cohomology classes of antisymmetric Bowen functions will be denoted as
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim
.

Note that
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim\subset\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim
is closed, thus a Banach space itself.

Altogether, this construction defines a map

\Phi:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim\qquad\qquad\Phi([L_{\mathfrak{g}}])=[\varphi_{\bm{L}}].

given as

[L_{\mathfrak{g}}]\mapsto[L_{\mathfrak{t}}|h^{-1}({E}_{0})]\mapsto[L|\Sigma_{0}]\mapsto[\bm{L}]\mapsto[\varphi_{L}].

Lemma 8.3
.

The map
\Phi
is a Banach isomorphism.

Linearity is immediate from the construction, and continuity is obtained by following the norms during the construction. It is also injective, due to
Lemma

8.1
and the discussion after its proof.

It remains to show surjectivity. Consider the Banach isomorphism
\Omega
obtained concatenating the maps
\Gamma_{1}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\widetilde{\mu}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma})/\thicksim
and
\Gamma^{-1}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\widetilde{\mu}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim
and
h_{*}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim
given by
Proposition

5.25
,
Corollary

5.29
and
Corollary

2.17
. Observe that
\Omega\left(\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(/)\thicksim\right)\subset\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
, and moreover for
[\varphi]\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(/)\thicksim
,
\Phi(\Omega([\varphi]))
and
[\varphi]
have the same integrals with respect to all periodic measures supported on points in
\Sigma_{0}
. Since periodic measures supported on
\Sigma_{0}
determine the cohomology class (see
Lemma

8.1
), it follows that
\Phi(\Omega([\varphi]))=[\varphi]
, and the proof is complete.
∎

Let
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
. Apply successively the Banach isomorphisms

•

\Ker\left(c_{2}:H^{2}_{b}(M;\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(M;\mathbb{R})\right)\to\frac{\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Hom}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})}
(
Proposition

3.3
),

•

B_{*}:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)\to\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))
(
Theorem

3.8
), and

•

\Phi:\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}^{\,a}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\mathfrak{g}))\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,a}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim

given above to conclude the claim.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

In the proof above,
[L]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
is trivial if and only if for every periodic orbit
\alpha
of
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathrm{av}_{\alpha}(\Psi([L]))=0
. This is consequence of
Theorem

E
.

Now we consider
Theorem

1.7
.

Define
X_{\Gamma}=\Sigma(R),{F}_{n}={B}^{n}
. Identify
\mathcal{R}(\Gamma;S)\approx\mathrm{Conj}(\Gamma)
and proceed as before to obtain a bijection

S:\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}_{0})\to\Sigma_{0}
, where
\Sigma_{0}
consists of a dense set of periodic points. For each
\alpha\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})\setminus{E}_{0}
we choose

\bm{a}\in\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({W})
so that the periodic orbit of
\mathfrak{t}
determined by
\bm{a}
is mapped onto
\alpha
by
h
. Altogether, we have a injective map
\Phi:(\Gamma;S)\to X_{\Gamma}
, whose image is
\mathrm{Per}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({W})
. Note that if
w\in(\Gamma;S)
then the period of
\Phi(w)
is uniformly comparable with its translation length, thus with
|w|_{S}
. This implies condition
2
of the Theorem.

Consider the finite measures
\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{\#B_{n}}\sum_{w\in B_{n}}\delta_{w}
and use
\Phi
to define corresponding periodic measures
\nu_{n}^{\prime}\in{P}_{\tau}(X_{\Gamma})
. By using the results of
Section

6.2
we conclude that
(\nu_{n}^{\prime})_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu^{\Gamma}:=\mu_{\mathrm{MME}}
, the entropy maximizing measure of
\tau
, which is well known to be a Markov measure (see next Section). This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Note that
\mu^{\Gamma}
as given above in principle depends on
S
. However, choosing
S^{\prime}
another (symmetric) finite generating set for
\Gamma
and carrying the construction, one sees first that the geodesic flows are equivalent, while the length of the periodic orbits is uniformly comparable. From this we get that we can use the same space
X_{\Gamma}
for both flows, and also that the corresponding periodic measures have the same limit measure
\mu^{\Gamma}
. On the other hand, the entropy maximizing measure of the flows are typically different.

For the proof of
Theorem

B
(and of its generalization to negatively curved spaces), we need some preparations. The topological pressure for the flow
\mathfrak{g}
corresponding to the potential
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})
is the number

\Ptop(\phi):=\sup_{\mathrm{m}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}({E})}\left(h_{\mathrm{m}}(\mathfrak{g})+\mathrm{m}(\phi)\right)

and we say
\nu
is an equilibrium measure for the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\phi)
if the supremum is attained at
\nu
. As discussed previously, if there exists an equilibrium measure then there exist an ergodic equilibrium measure. Similar definitions hold for the suspension flow
\mathfrak{t}
.

As in the last part of
Section

5
(see (
5.3
)) consider

\widetilde{\phi}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=\int_{0}^{f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

where we are tacitly assuming that
\psi
is integrable with respect to the variable
s
: this is no loss of generality, because
\psi
is cohomologous a function satisfying this property, and equilibrium measures for cohomologous potentials are the same.

Recall that
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly positive. From this, and arguing identically as in Proposition
3.1
of
[
65
]
we obtain:

Proposition 8.6

(Bowen and Ruelle)
.

It holds that
\Ptop(\phi)=c
, where
c
is the unique solution to the equation
\Ptop(\widetilde{\phi}-c\cdot f)=0
. Moreover,
\nu
is an equilibrium measure for
\phi
if and only its corresponding measure
\mu
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is an equilibrium measure for
\tilde{\phi}-\Ptop(\phi)\cdot f
.

Remark 8.7
.

In
[
65
]
the proof is given assuming some regularity of
\phi
. This property is only used for the uniqueness of equilibrium states for
\widetilde{\phi}
, which is true in our context.

We bring to the attention of the reader that
Theorem

A
(
Theorem

2.11
) implies in particular that for every ergodic measure
\widetilde{\nu}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}({E})
of full support, the spaces
\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim,\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\widetilde{\nu}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim
are Banach isomorphic; a similar statement holds for the suspension flow
\mathfrak{t}
.

In particular, due to
Corollary

2.17
we get:

Lemma 8.8
.

There exists a Banach isomorphism
\Gamma_{3}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim
.

From this we deduce:

Corollary 8.9
.

Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E}),\phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})
so that
\Gamma_{3}([\phi])=[\phi^{\prime}]
.

Then
(\mathfrak{g},\phi)
has a unique equilibrium state if and only if
(\mathfrak{t},\phi^{\prime})
has a unique equilibrium state.

We fix some reference (ergodic) measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}\in{P}_{\mathfrak{g}}({E})
of full support, and consider the Banach isomorphism
\Psi:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim\approx\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)/\thicksim
.

Given
\phi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})
take
\varphi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
so that
\Psi([\phi-\Ptop(\phi)f])=[\varphi]
. As discussed above,
\mu_{\varphi}\in{P}_{\tau}(\Sigma)
is the equilibrium state of
\varphi
if and only if its extension to
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}

Equation

2.4
is the unique equilibrium state of
\phi
. This proves uniqueness of the equilibrium state.

The Bernoulli part concerning equilibrium measures is consequence of
Corollary

7.36
, since this implies that if
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}
is a given equilibrium measure for a weak Bowen potential for the suspension flow, then
(\mathfrak{t},\nu_{\mathfrak{t}})
is a Bernoulli flow. Since
h:(\mathfrak{t},\nu)\to(\mathfrak{g},\nu_{\mathfrak{g}})
is a metric isomorphism, we deduce the same for the later system.
∎

9.
The Central Limit Theorem for Bowen quasimorphisms and statistics for the Patterson-Sullivan measure

The last two sections are devoted to the statistical properties of quasimorphisms. In this part we prove
Theorems

C
and
D
.

We start introducing some notation. Fix
\varphi:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
a function with the Bowen property and denote
{L}_{\varphi}
its corresponding transfer operator. We assume that
{L}_{\varphi}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}=\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}
, and consider its associated stationary measure
\mu_{\varphi}
:
\mu_{\varphi}
is invariant and is the unique equilibrium state corresponding to the system
(\varphi,\tau)
(Cf.
[
14
]
and compare with
Corollary

6.26
).

We now specialize in measures associated to Markov potentials.

Definition 9.1
.

\varphi:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
is called a Markov potential of memory
s\geq 0
if it is constant on cylinders of size
s+1
. The space of Markov potentials of memory
s
is denoted by
\mathrm{LC}_{s}
. The associated equilibrium state is called a Markov measure.

Example 9.2
.

The zero potential
\varphi_{0}\equiv 0
is clearly a Markov potential of memory
0
. Its associated equilibrium state is
\mu=\mu_{\mathrm{MME}}
, the entropy maximizing measure of
\tau
(also called the Parry measure of
\tau
). See
[
69
]
.

We remark however that
\varphi_{0}
is not normalized, that is
{L}_{\varphi}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}\neq\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}
. Instead, denoting
\lambda=e^{\htop(\tau)}
, there exists some function
u:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
which only depends on the first coordinate, and so that

\varphi(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=u(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})-u(\tau\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})-\htop(\tau)=u(x_{0})-u(x_{1})-\htop(\tau)

is the (unique) normalized potential cohomologous to
\varphi_{0}
(in particular, its equilibrium state is
\mu_{\mathrm{MME}}
).

In general, it is a consequence of the Perron-Frobenius-Ruelle theorem that any
\varphi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}
is cohomologous to some normalized Markov potential
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LC}_{s+1}
. With no loss of generality, we assume that
\varphi
is a normalized Markov potential of memory
s
.

Theorem 9.3
.

For every
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{\varphi}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
with
\int\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}=0
, there is
h\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)
such that
(\Id-\mu_{\varphi}h=\psi
. Moreover
\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\psi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
, for some constant
\Lambda(\varphi)
.

Corollary 9.4
.

For every
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{\varphi}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
with
\int\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}=0
, there is
\overline{h}\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)
with
\lVert\overline{h}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{(}s+1)\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\psi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
and such that
\overline{h}\circ\tau-\overline{h}-\psi\in\Ker{L}_{\varphi}
.

Let
h
be as in the theorem and
\overline{h}={L}_{\varphi}h
, then

{L}_{\varphi}(({L}_{\varphi}h)\circ\tau-{L}_{\varphi}h-\psi)=(\Id-{L}_{\varphi}h){L}_{\varphi}h-{L}_{\varphi}\psi={L}_{\varphi}\left((\Id-{L}_{\varphi})h-\psi\right)=0.

∎

We now prove
Theorem

9.3
.

Lemma 9.5
.

{L}_{\varphi}(\mathrm{LC}_{s})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s}
and
{L}_{\varphi}(\mathrm{LC}_{s+k})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s+k-1}
for every
k\geq 1
.

In particular for
m\geq 0
,
{L}_{\varphi}^{m}(\mathrm{LC}_{s+k})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s+k-m}
for every
k\geq m
and, if
k\leq m
then
{L}_{\varphi}^{m}(\mathrm{LC}_{s+k})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s}
. Thus, for every
N\geq 1
it holds
{L}_{\varphi}^{N-1}(\mathrm{LC}_{N})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s}
.

Compute directly,

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{s}]}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=\sum_{j}e^{\varphi(j\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{s}]}(j\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=e^{\varphi(w_{1}x_{0}\dots x_{s-1})}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{2}\dots w_{s}]}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})

and

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}(\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{s+k}]})(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=\sum_{j}e^{\varphi(j\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{s+k}]}(j\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=e^{\varphi(w_{1}x_{0}\dots x_{s-1})}\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[w_{2}\dots w_{s+k}]}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}).

∎

The subspace
\mathrm{LC}_{s}\subset L^{2}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)
is closed. Let
P_{s}^{\perp},P_{s}=\Id-P_{s}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal projections of the splitting
L^{2}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)=\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{\perp}\oplus\mathrm{LC}_{s}
.

Remark 9.6
.

We remind the reader that

P_{s}(\rho)=\sum_{|\bm{w}|=s}\left(\frac{1}{\mu_{\varphi}([\bm{w}])}\int_{[\bm{w}]}\rho\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}\right)\mathchoice{\text{1}}{\text{1}}{\scalebox{0.7}{\text{1}}}{\scalebox{0.5}{\text{1}}}_{[\bm{w}]}=\mathbb{E}_{#1}[\rho|\xi^{s}].

Lemma 9.7
.

P^{\perp}_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}\circ P^{\perp}_{s}=P^{\perp}_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}
.

It holds that
P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}={L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}
, since
{L}_{\varphi}
preserves
\mathrm{LC}_{s}
. Thus

\displaystyle(\Id-P_{s})\circ{L}_{\varphi}\circ(\Id-P_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}\circ(\Id-P_{s})-P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}\circ(\Id-P_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}-{L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}-P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}+P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}-{L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}-P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}+{L}_{\varphi}\circ P_{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{L}_{\varphi}-P_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}=P^{\perp}_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}.

∎

Letting
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}=P^{\perp}_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi},
we get for every
N\geq 1
,

\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N}=P^{\perp}_{s}\circ{L}_{\varphi}^{N}.

Lemma 9.8
.

For every
N\geq 1
,
\mathrm{LC}_{N}\subset\Ker\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}
.

Since
{L}_{\varphi}^{N-1}(\mathrm{LC}_{N})\subset\mathrm{LC}_{s}
, we get
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}(\mathrm{LC}_{N})=P^{\perp}_{s}{L}_{\varphi}^{N-1}(\mathrm{LC}_{N})=\{0\}
.
∎

It is not difficult to check that for every
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
\lVert{L}_{\varphi}:L^{p}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)\toit\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{OP}}=1
. On the other hand, the conditional expectation
P_{s}
extends to a linear map
P_{s}:L^{p}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)\toit
of norm one, hence we can also extend
P_{s}^{\perp}:=\Id-P_{s}:L^{p}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)\toit
. It follows in particular that
\lVert P_{s}^{\perp}:L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{OP}}\leq 2
.

Proposition 9.9
.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{\varphi}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
, then for every
N\geq 1
,

\lVert\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi\right)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}.

Let us write

S_{N}\psi=\left(S_{N}\psi-P_{N}(S_{N}\psi)\right)+P_{N}(S_{N}\psi)

and note that
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}(P_{N}(S_{N}\psi))=0
. On the other hand, by the Bowen property, we get that

\lVert S_{N}\psi-P_{N}(S_{N}\psi)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}{\infty}\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B},

hence

\displaystyle\lVert\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi\right)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}=\lVert P_{s}^{\perp}{L}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi-P_{N}(S_{N}\psi)\right)\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}.

∎

Recall that we are denoting by
T
the Koopman operator of
\tau
. The transference property of
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}
now gives:

Lemma 9.10
.

It holds that for
k\geq 0
,
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{n}\circ T^{k}=\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{n-k}
for
N>k
and
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N}\circ T^{N}=P_{s}^{\perp}
. Hence for
N\geq 1
,

\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi\right)=P_{s}^{\perp}\psi+\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{k}\psi=P_{s}^{\perp}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{L}_{\varphi}^{k}\psi\right).

Compute

\displaystyle\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(T^{k}\psi\right)=P_{s}^{\perp}\psi+\sum_{k=0}^{N-2}\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1-k}\psi=P_{s}^{\perp}\psi+\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{k}\psi

\displaystyle=P_{s}^{\perp}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{L}_{\varphi}^{k}\psi\right).

∎

For
N\geq 1
define
u_{N}=\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}^{N-1}\left(S_{N}\psi\right)
: then
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}u_{N}=u_{N+1}-P_{s}^{\perp}\psi
, and by the previous Proposition,
\lVert u_{N}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
. Denote

h_{M}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{N=1}^{M}u_{N}.

By direct computation,

\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}h_{M}=h_{M}-P_{s}^{\perp}\psi-\frac{u_{N+1}-u_{1}}{M}.

Now take
h
any weak-
L^{2}
accumulation point of
(h_{M})_{M}
. Using that
\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}
is weakly continuous (in fact, strongly continuous), we deduce

\widehat{{L}}_{\varphi}h=h-P_{s}^{\perp}\psi.

This tells us in particular that
h=P_{s}^{\perp}\left({L}_{\varphi}h+\psi\right)
, hence
P_{s}^{\perp}h=h
and so

P_{s}^{\perp}\left({L}_{\varphi}h-h+\psi\right)=0\qquad{L}_{\varphi}h-h+\psi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}.

Remark 9.11
.

By construction,
\int h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=0
.

Lemma 9.12
.

\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
.

Indeed, for every
M\geq 1
we have that
\lVert h_{M}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
. Since the weak limit of non-negative functions is non-negative, we get that the the
L^{\infty}
norm of
h
is bounded by
2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
.
∎

Putting everything together, we have proved:

Corollary 9.13
.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu_{\varphi}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
then there exists
h\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{\perp}
verifying:

(1)

{L}_{\varphi}h-h+\psi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}
.

(2)

\lVert L^{\infty}\rVert{h}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}
.

The projection on the locally constant part

Dealing with functions in
\mathrm{LC}_{s}
is essentially linear algebra. Denote
\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}=\{\phi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}:\int\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}=0\}
.

Lemma 9.14
.

{L}_{\varphi}:\mathrm{LC}_{s}\toit
is a Perron-Frobenius operator with eigenvalue
1
and eigenfunction
1
, and so
\Id-{L}_{\varphi}|_{\mathrm{LC}^{0}_{s}}
is invertible. If
\rho\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}
then

\lVert(\Id-{L}_{\varphi})^{-1}\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}\leq\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}

The fact that
{L}_{\varphi}
is a Perron-Frobenius operator (equivalently, an stochastic matrix on the
d\cdot(s+1)-
dimensional vector space
\mathrm{LC}_{s}
) is direct, and thus by its spectral resolution we get the invertibility of
\Id-{L}_{\varphi}
on the complementary of the eigenspace associated to the Perron eigenvalue. For a square matrix
A
denote
\lVert A\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{OP}}
its operator norm with respect to the
\ell^{\infty}
norm on the vector space, and
\lVert A\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{sp}}
its spectral norm. Let
\lambda_{2}
be the second largest eigenvalue of
{L}_{\varphi}
:
\lambda_{2}
is strictly smaller than
1
, hence for
\rho\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert{L}_{\varphi}^{n}\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert{L}_{\varphi}^{n}|_{\mathrm{LC}^{0}_{s}}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{OP}}\cdot\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}\leq\sqrt{d\cdot(s+1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lVert{L}_{\varphi}^{n}|_{\mathrm{LC}^{0}_{s}}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{sp}}\cdot\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{s+1}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{1-\lambda_{2}}\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}.

Taking
\Lambda(\varphi)=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{1-\lambda_{2}}
it follows that
\lVert(\Id-{L}_{\varphi})^{-1}\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}\leq\sqrt{s+1}\Lambda(\varphi)\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}
.
∎

We are to prove that any
\psi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}
is Livšic cohomologous to an element of
\Ker{L}_{\varphi}
.

For
\psi\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}
let
\rho={L}_{\varphi}h-h+\psi
. Then
\rho\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}
, so there is
h_{1}\in\mathrm{LC}_{s}^{0}
such that
h_{1}-{L}_{\varphi}h_{1}=\rho
. Letting
h_{0}=-h+h_{1}
, it follows that

\displaystyle h_{0}-{L}_{\varphi}h_{0}

\displaystyle=-h+{L}_{\varphi}h+h_{1}-{L}_{\varphi}h_{1}=\psi-\rho+h_{1}-{L}_{\varphi}h_{1}=\psi,

and moreover

\displaystyle\lVert h_{0}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\lVert h_{1}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}{\infty}\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}+\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\lVert\rho\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}+\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}(2\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}})\leq 2\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}+\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\left(4\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+4\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\right)\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}+\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 5\Lambda(\varphi)\sqrt{s+1}\lvert\!\lvert\!\lvert\psi\rvert\!\rvert\!\rvert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!B}.

∎

Properties of the variance

At this stage we can use some classical machinery initially developed by Gordin to establish the CLT for dynamical system, based mostly on the CLT for martingales differences of Billingsley. We rely on Brown’s version
[
70
]
; a thorough exposition is given in the book by Hall and Heyde
[
71
]
. To simplify the notation now
\mu
denotes a fixed Markov measure, and

{L}={L}_{\varphi}
the corresponding transfer operator.

Fix a locally constant quasicocycle
L=(L^{(n)})_{n}
, and consider its associated weak Bowen function
\varphi_{L}=\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mu}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Sigma)
as given by
Proposition

6.30
. Denote
\psi_{L}=\varphi_{L}-\mu(L)
, and we remind the reader that changing
L
by a cohomologous cocycle has the effect of changing
\psi_{L}
by a Livšic cohomologous potential, which also has the weak Bowen property. Define

(9.1)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(L)=\limsup_{n}\frac{1}{n}\lVert S_{n}\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

From the previous line one gets that
\sigma^{2}
is constant on cohomology classes. Let
\widetilde{\psi}_{L}\in\Ker{L}
be as in
Corollary

9.4
, hence
\psi_{L}=\widetilde{\psi}_{L}+v-v\circ\tau
for some
v\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu\right)
. It follows that there exists some constant
E>0
independent of
N
so that
\mu\text{-a.e.}
,

(9.2)

\displaystyle|L^{(N)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})-S_{N}\widetilde{\psi}_{L}-N\mu(L)|\leq E.

On the other hand, for
\psi\in L^{2}\!\left(\mu\right)

(9.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\lVert S_{N}\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}}{N}=\lVert\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{n}{N}\right)\langle\psi\circ\tau^{n},\psi\rangle

hence for
\psi=\widetilde{\psi}_{L}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\lVert S_{N}\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}}{N}=\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

and

\sigma^{2}(L)=\lVert\widetilde{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}.

Corollary 9.15
.

If
L
verifies
\mu(L)=0
, then
\sigma(L)=0
if and only if
L
is cohomologically trivial.

Indeed,
\sigma(L)=0
if and only if
\varphi_{L}=\psi_{L}=v-v\circ\tau
in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Since
\psi_{L}
has the weak Bowen property, this holds if and only if
\psi_{L}
is a
L^{\infty}-
coboundary with
L^{\infty}
transfer function (
Theorem

5.19
). This implies the claim.
∎

Before moving further, we establish some properties of the variance, which are of interest. We have that
\psi_{L}=h-{L}h
, where
h\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. Recall (
Remark

9.11
) that
\int h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=0
.

Lemma 9.16
.

For every
u\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu_{\varphi}\right)
with
\int u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\varphi}=0
it holds
\lVert{L}^{n}u\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{\infty}}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}0
.

Indeed, the uniformly bounded sequence of operators
\{{L}^{n}:L^{\infty}_{0}(\mu)\to L^{\infty}_{0}(\mu)\}
converges pointwise to zero for every continuous function
u\in L^{\infty}_{0}(\mu)=\{v\in L^{\infty}\!\left(\mu\right),\mu(u)=0\}
, therefore everywhere.
∎

The above permits us to identify the function
h
in
L^{p}\!\left(\mu\right)
.

Lemma 9.17
.

For every
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
h\mathrel{\underset{\mathclap{L^{p}}}{=}}\sum_{n\geq 0}{L}^{n}\psi_{L}
, i.e.
\lVert h-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}{L}^{n}\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{p}}\xrightarrow[N\mapsto\infty]{}0
.

\displaystyle h-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}{L}^{n}\psi_{L}

\displaystyle=h-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}{L}^{n}(h-{L}^{n}h)=h-\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}{L}^{n}h-\sum_{n=1}^{N}{L}^{n}h\right)

\displaystyle=h-\left(h-{L}^{N}h\right)={L}^{N}h\xrightarrow[N\mapsto\infty]{L^{p}\!\left(\mu\right)}0.

∎

In particular we get:

Corollary 9.18
.

For every
\rho\in L^{1}\!\left(\mu\right)
, it holds that
(\int\psi_{L}\cdot\rho\circ\tau^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu)_{n}
is summable and the sum is

\sum_{n\geq 0}\int\psi_{L}\cdot\rho\circ\tau^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int h\rho\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu.

Next we get a formula for the variance.

Corollary 9.19
.

It holds that
\sigma^{2}_{\mu}(\psi_{L})=\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\int\psi\cdot\psi\circ\tau^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
.

Compute

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{\mu}(\psi_{L})

\displaystyle=\lVert\overline{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert\psi+{L}h-\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert h-\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}-2\langle h,\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau\rangle

\displaystyle=\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}-2\langle{L}h,{L}h\rangle=\lVert h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}-\lVert{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\psi_{L}+{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}-\lVert{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle\psi_{L},{L}h\rangle=\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\langle\psi_{L},{L}^{n}\psi_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\langle\psi_{L},\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{n}\rangle.

∎

We also deduce the usual formula:

Lemma 9.20
.

\frac{1}{n}\lVert S_{n}\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\sigma^{2}_{\mu}(\psi_{L})=\lVert\overline{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}
.

For every
n
,

S_{n}\psi_{L}=S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L}+\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h,

so

\displaystyle\lVert S_{n}\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L},\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h\rangle

\displaystyle=n\lVert\overline{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\langle S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L},\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}\rangle-2\langle S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L},{L}h\rangle

\displaystyle=n\lVert\overline{\psi}_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}-2\langle S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L},{L}h\rangle

since
\overline{\psi}_{L}\in\Ker{L}
implies that
{L}^{n}\left(S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L}\right)=0
. Now, on the one hand, we have that
\lVert\left({L}h\right)\circ\tau^{n}-{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}\leq 2\lVert{L}h\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}
. Since
\int\overline{\psi}_{L}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=0
, by Von Neumann ergodic theorem the sequence
\frac{1}{n}S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L}
goes to zero in
L^{2}\!\left(\mu\right)
and hence we get that
\frac{1}{n}\langle S_{n}\overline{\psi}_{L},{L}h\rangle\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}0
, so the lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 9.21
.

It holds

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}k\int\psi_{L}\cdot\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\xrightarrow[N\mapsto\infty]{}0

and hence
(n\int\psi_{L}\cdot\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu)_{n\geq 0}
tends to
0
in the Cesàro sense.

By
Corollary

9.19
and (
9.3
) we get

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\psi_{L})=\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n\geq 1}\langle\psi_{L},\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{n}\rangle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\lVert S_{N}\psi\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}}{N}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\lVert\psi_{L}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}^{2}+2\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\left(1-\frac{n}{N}\right)\langle\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{n},\psi_{L}\rangle\right),

which proves the Lemma.
∎

Remark 9.22
.

Convergence of the series
\sum_{n\geq 1}n\int\psi_{L}\cdot\psi_{L}\circ\tau^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
remains to be proven. If this were the case one would get that
(S_{n}\psi_{L}-n\sigma^{2}(\psi_{L}))_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}\!\left(\mu\right)
.

Cohomology to a martingale difference

After this preparations we move to the proof of the Central Limit Theorem. Assume, with no loss of generality, that
\mu(L)=0
and
\sigma:=\sigma(L)\neq 0
. Since
0={L}\psi_{L}(\tau\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=\mathbb{E}_{#1}[\psi_{L}|\tau^{-1}{B}(\Sigma)](\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})
, it follows that
\left(S_{n}\psi_{L},\tau^{-n}{B}(\Sigma)\right)_{n\geq 0}
is a reverse Martingale, therefore by the Martingale Central Limit Theorem
[
70
]
we get

\mu\left(\frac{S_{n}\psi_{L}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Lemma 9.23
.

It holds

\mu\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and write
r_{n}=\frac{L^{(n)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})-S_{n}\psi_{L}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}
, so that
\lVert r_{n}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!L^{2}}\leq\frac{E}{\sqrt{n}}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\mu\left(r_{n}>\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{E^{2}}{\epsilon n}.

Then for
c\in\mathbb{R}
fixed,

\displaystyle\mu\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(\frac{S_{n}\psi_{L}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c-\varepsilon\right)+\mu(r_{n}>\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c-\epsilon}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\limsup_{n}\mu\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

and likewise

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\mu\left(\frac{L^{(n)}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

which implies the claim.
∎

By the same argument one obtains:

Lemma 9.24
.

If
\psi_{L}\sim\psi_{L}^{\prime}
, then for all
c\in\mathbb{R}

\mu\left(\frac{S_{n}\psi_{L}^{\prime}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Next consider the natural extension
\pi:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
. The unique lift of
\mu
to
\Sigma
is the equilibrium state of
\varphi\circ\pi
, and will be denoted as
\widetilde{\mu}

Lemma 9.25
.

Let
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\widetilde{\mu}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma})
be a function with zero integral. Then for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\widetilde{\mu}\left(\frac{S_{n}\psi}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Using
Proposition

5.25
and the previous Lemma one gets that it is enough to prove the same for
\psi_{L}
only depending on positive coordinates. This is checked directly, remembering that convergence in distribution for a sequence
(\mathrm{m}_{n})_{n}\in{P}(\mathbb{R})
to
\mathrm{m}
is equivalent to convergence of
(\mathrm{m}_{n}(k))_{n}
to
\mathrm{m}(k)
, for every bounded continuous function
k:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. ∎

We use the
Theorem

1.7
, where
X_{\Gamma}=\Sigma
and consider
\mu=\mu_{\mathrm{MME}}
.
Corollary

C
was proven in
Lemma

9.23
. As for
Theorem

D
, we write

\displaystyle S_{n}=S_{n}\psi_{L}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{t}(n,x)=\frac{S_{([nt])}(x)+(nt-[nt])\psi_{L}(\tau^{[nt]+1})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}

Then
(S_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an stationary ergodic sequence cohomologous to
(L^{(n)})_{n\geq 1}
. As noted above
(S_{n},\tau^{-n}{B}(\Sigma))
is a reverse martingale with uniformly bounded differences, hence Donsker invariance principle and the Law of iterated logarithm holds for this sequence. See
[
70
]
for the first part and Corollary
4.1
in
[
71
]
for the second. This establishes the first two parts of
Theorem

D
.

The last part is direct consequence of the concentration inequalities for uniformly bounded Martingale differences. See for example Corollary
2.4.7
in

[
72
]
.
∎

CLT for flows

We now address flows.

Notation
.

We denote by
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}},\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}
the corresponding measure on
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f},{E}
for the flows
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{g}
.

Definition 9.26
.

An invariant measure
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}
for
\mathfrak{g}
is said to be Markov if the corresponding measure on the symbolic model is a Markov measure.

Since we working with
\mu=\mu_{\mathrm{MME}}
,
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}
is Markov. If
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mathfrak{t}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}),T>0
then

\int_{0}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=S_{n}\widetilde{\psi}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})+\int_{n}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

where
n
is the unique natural number so that
S_{n}f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})\leq T<S_{n+1}f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})
, and
\widetilde{\psi}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})=\int_{0}^{f(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t
(cf. (
5.3
)).

Due to
Lemma

9.25
we deduce the CLT for
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}-
Bowen functions.

Corollary 9.27
.

If
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\mathfrak{t}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})
is not a coboundary, then there exists some
\sigma^{2}>0
so that

\forall c\in\mathbb{R},\ \nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\left([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s]:\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s+t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-T\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}(\psi)}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{(u-\gamma)^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

With no loss of generality assume
\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}(\psi)=0
: let
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}(\widetilde{\psi})>0
. Note that the hypothesis and
Lemma

5.27
imply that
\tilde{\psi}
is not a coboundary.

For
\varepsilon>0
, and arguing analogously as in
Lemma

9.24
, we get that

\displaystyle\limsup_{T\to\infty}\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\left([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s]:\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s+t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\leq c\right)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{\mu}\left([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s]:\frac{S_{n}\psi}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c-\epsilon\right),

hence by
Lemma

9.25

\displaystyle\limsup_{T\to\infty}\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\left([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s]:\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s+t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\leq c\right)\leq\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{(u-\gamma)^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Similarly,
\liminf_{T\to\infty}\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}\left([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s]:\frac{\int_{0}^{T}\psi([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},s+t])\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\leq c\right)\geq\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{(u-\gamma)^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
.
∎

Recall that the semi-conjugacy
h:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\to{E}
induces an isomorphism
h^{*}:\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})/\thicksim\to\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f})/\thicksim
. Fix
\psi\in\mathrm{Bow}^{\,}_{\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}({E})
that is not a coboundary and verifies
\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}(\psi)=0
. Let
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}(h^{*}[\psi])
and denote

\displaystyle X_{T}(x)=\frac{S_{T}^{\mathfrak{g}}\psi(x)}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle Y_{T}([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])=\frac{S_{T}^{\mathfrak{t}}\psi\circ h([\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}},t])}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}

Since
h(g_{t})=\tau_{t}(h)
,
h\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}=\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}
, we get
X_{T}\nu_{\mathfrak{g}}=Y_{T}\nu_{\mathfrak{t}}
, while by the Corollary above we know that
(Y_{T}\nu_{\mathfrak{t}})_{T>0}
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 9.28
.

It would be interesting to establish the CLT for Bowen functions, when the equilibrium state is associated to non-Markov potentials. Particularly, the case of the potential
\varphi:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{f}\to\mathbb{R},\varphi=-\htop(\mathfrak{g})f
corresponds to the entropy maximizing measure of
\mathfrak{g}
. When
M
is a closed hyperbolic manifold this is the same as the Liouville measure on
T^{1}M
.

9.1.
CLT for quasimorphisms with respect to the Patterson-Sullivan measure and spherical means

We now reap some corollaries of the previous work, and obtain asymptotic information for unbounded quasimorphisms
[L]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
with respect to the Patterson-Sullivan measure
\nu\in{P}(\partial\Gamma)
. To do this, we combine the general limit theorems of the previous part with techniques originally developed by Calegari-Fujiwara
[
28
]
, and further developed by Cantrell
[
30
,
73
]
(see also Cantrell-Sert
[
33
]
) to study regular (e.g. combable) quasimorphisms. The content of this part was suggested to us by S. Cantrell.

The latter three articles even give Barry-Esseen type inequalities. Here we extend some of their results to arbitrary unbounded quasimorphisms, but we lose information on the speed of convergence. We will be following mainly
[
73
]
, where the computations are written in terms of uniform asymptotic estimates which are not present in our context. For convenience of the reader, we sketch the relevant parts in our context.

Take
\Gamma
a non-elementary hyperbolic group (say,
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(M,*)
where
M
is a closed locally
\mathrm{CAT}\!\left(-a^{2}\right)
space), and fix
S
a finite symmetric generating set. We will use a different type of symbolic model for
\Gamma
and
\partial\Gamma
.

Theorem 9.29

(Cannon coding,
[
74
]
)
.

There exists a finite graph
(V,E)
(vertices, edges) and
l:E\to S
(the labeling map) so that

(1)

there exists an initial vertex
*\in V
;

(2)

Finite paths
*\mapsto x_{1}\mapsto x_{2}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto x_{n}
in the graph are in bijection with finite words in
S
of the form

\displaystyle l(*,x_{1})l(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots l(x_{n-1}x_{n})

\displaystyle|l(*,x_{1})l(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots l(x_{n-1}x_{n})|_{S}=n.

In order to be able to represent elements of
\Gamma
by finite words in
\Sigma(A)
, it is convenient to add and additional vertex
0\not\in V_{S}
together with vertices
(v,0)
for every
v\in V_{S}\setminus\{*\}
: the function
l
is extended as
l(v,0)=1_{\Gamma}
. Consider the resulting graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
, and let
A\in\Mat_{|\mathcal{V}|}(\{0,1\})
be its adjacency matrix. We consider
(\Sigma(A),\tau)
the corresponding dynamical system: note however that this is not irreducible in our definition. On the other hand, if
\lambda=e^{\htop(\tau)}
is the spectral radius of
A
, then there exist square sub-matrices
B
(of
A
) with spectral radius equal to
\lambda
, and each such sub-matrix verifies that
\tau|:\Sigma(B)\subset\Sigma(A)\to\Sigma(B)
is transitive. Denote by
\{B_{1},\cdots,B_{k}\}
all these transitive components. We remark that
\tau|:\Sigma(B_{i})\to\Sigma(B_{i})
has a unique measure of maximal entropy
\mu_{i}
, which is a Markov measure.

There is a natural embedding
\iota:\Gamma\to\Sigma(A)
,
\iota(g)=(*,x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},0,0,\cdots)
where

\displaystyle g=l(*,x_{1})l(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots l(x_{n-1}x_{n})

\displaystyle|l(*,x_{1})l(x_{1},x_{2})\cdots l(x_{n-1}x_{n})|_{S}=n.

Also, if
A^{\prime}
is the matrix obtained from
A
by removing the row and column corresponding to
v=0
, and
Y=\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in\Sigma_{A^{\prime}}:x_{0}=*\}
, then there is a bijection
h:Y\to\partial\Gamma
. If
n\in\mathbb{N},\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in Y
, we write
h(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})_{n}
for the initial segment of size
n
of the geodesic ray
h(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})
.

Proposition 9.30

(
[
28
]
)
.

There exists a unique measure
\hat{\nu}
on
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
so that
h\hat{\nu}=\nu
. In particular,
\hat{\nu}
is supported on
Y
.

Let

\mu_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\tau^{k}\hat{\nu}.

Proposition 9.31

(Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.2
in
[
73
]
)
.

The sequence
(\mu_{N})
converges to a measure
\mu
, which is a convex combination of the measures
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{k}
.

This measure
\mu
is supported on
X=\bigcup_{i}^{k}\Sigma(B_{i})
. The idea is then comparing the measure
\hat{\nu}
with the limit measure
\mu
: one of the main difficulties to overcome is that these measure are supported in different sets of
\Sigma(A)
.

Fix
0\neq[L]\in\widetilde{\mathrm{QM}}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(\Gamma)
, which with no-loss of generality we assume to be homogeneous. The Patterson–Sullivan can be obtained as

\displaystyle\nu=\lim_{n}\nu_{n},\quad\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{\#S_{n}}\sum_{g\in B_{n}}\delta_{g}

where
B_{n}=\{g\in\Gamma:|g|_{S}\leq n\}

[
75
]
. We use
\iota
to lift the measures
\nu_{n}
to measures
\eta_{n}
on
Y
: it is direct to check that
(\eta_{n})_{n}
converges weakly to
\hat{\nu}
. Similarly, by identifying
Y=\Sigma(A^{\prime})
one uses
L
to define
L\in\mathrm{QM}\mkern-1.0mu\mathopen{}(Y)
, and considers
\bm{L}=\{L^{(n)}:Y\to\mathbb{R}\}_{n}
the corresponding locally constant quasicocycle.

Since
B_{n}
is symmetric and
L
is homogeneous, it follows that for every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\eta_{n+m}(L^{(n)})=0
, and thus

\hat{\nu}(L^{(n)})=0
. Similarly, for every
j\geq 0
,
\hat{\nu}(L^{(n)}\circ\tau^{j})=0
. From this we deduce that
\mu(L^{(n)})=0
, and since
\mu
is invariant,
\mu(\bm{L})=0
, which in turn implies that for every
1\leq i\leq k
,
\mu_{i}(\bm{L})=0
as well.

For
\sigma\neq 0
denote
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma)
the centered normal distribution function with variance
\sigma^{2}
. As consequence of the results in the previous part we get:

Proposition 9.32
.

There exists
\sigma^{2}=\sigma^{2}(L)>0
so that for every
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{L^{(n)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{{D}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma)

Applying
Lemma

9.23
to each component
\Sigma_{i}
one deduces one of the two possibilities: either
L^{(n)}
is bounded on
\Sigma(B_{i})
, or
\left(\frac{L^{(n)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\right)_{n}
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{i})
, for some
\sigma_{i}
. If
\sigma_{i}
is zero, then one can show that the set
\{[r]\in\partial\Gamma:\sup_{n\geq\mathbb{N}}|L(r_{n})|<\infty\}
has positive
\nu-
measure, hence by ergodicity of
\nu
it implies that this set has full measure. A posteriori,
L
is bounded, which contradicts what we assumed. See for example Proposition
5.6
in
[
30
]
. This method is due to Calegari and Fujiwara
[
28
]
, where the ergodicity is
\nu
is used further to guarantee that all
\sigma_{i}
coincide (in page
17
of
[
30
]
substitute
f^{n}
by
L^{(n)}
), hence
\sigma_{i}=\sigma>0
.
∎

To continue, Proposition
4.6
in
[
73
]
shows that
\lVert\mu_{N}-\mu\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\mathrm{TV}}=O(N^{-1})
, therefore defining

E_{n}(c,X)=\left(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in X:\frac{L^{(n)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq t\right),

one gets:

Corollary 9.33
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N},c\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|\mu_{N}(E_{n}(c,X))-\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\right|\leq\xi_{n}(c)+O(N^{-1}).

where
\lim_{n\to\infty}\xi_{n}(t)=0
.

The following measures are introduced to compare
\mu_{N}(E_{n}(c,X))
with
\hat{\nu}(E_{n}(c,X))
. Consider the sets

\displaystyle Y_{i}=\{y\in Y:\exists m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }\forall n\geq m,\tau^{n}y\in\Sigma(B_{i})\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}=\bigcup_{i}Y_{i}

\displaystyle E_{n}(c,\widetilde{Y})=\left\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in\widetilde{Y}:\frac{L^{(n)}(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right\}.

Definition 9.34
.

For
j\in\mathbb{N}
let
A_{j}=\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in\Sigma(A^{\prime}):\tau^{k}y\notin X\forall k=0,\ldots,j-1\text{ and }\tau^{j}\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in X\}
. For
n\geq 1
define the measures

\hat{\nu}_{n}(E)=\hat{\nu}(E\cap\bigcap_{j=0}^{n}A_{j}).

Observe that if
E\subset X
then
\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}(E)=\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}_{j}(E)
.

Lemma 9.35

(Lemma
5.8
in
[
73
]
)
.

For every sequence
(N_{n})
,

\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{j=0}^{N_{n}-1}\hat{\nu}_{j}\left(E_{n}(t,\widetilde{Y})\right)=\hat{\nu}\left(E_{n}(t,\widetilde{Y})\right)+O(N_{n}^{-1})

Fix
N_{n}=[\sqrt[4]{n}]
and let
C>0
so that
\lVert L^{(n)}\rVert_{\scriptscriptstyle\!\ell^{\infty}}\leq Cn
. Define

\displaystyle C_{n}^{\pm}(c,X)=\left\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in X:\frac{L^{(n)}(y)}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\pm\frac{C}{\sqrt[4]{n}}\right\}

Then for
0\leq j\leq N_{n}
,

\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}(C_{n}^{-}(c,X))=\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}_{j}(C_{n}^{-}(c,X))\leq\hat{\nu}_{j}(E_{n}(c,\widetilde{Y}))\leq\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}_{j}(C_{n}^{+}(c,X))=\tau^{j}\hat{\nu}(C_{n}^{+}(c,X))

and

\mu_{N_{n}}(C_{n}^{-}(c,X))\leq\hat{\nu}\left(E_{n}(c,\widetilde{Y})\right)+O(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})\leq\mu_{N_{n}}(C_{n}^{+}(c,X)).

Corollary 9.36
.

For every
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lim_{n}\hat{\nu}\left(E_{n}(c,\widetilde{Y})\right)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
.

Direct consequence of
Corollary

9.33
and the previous inequality. ∎

We are ready to prove
Corollary

D
, starting with its first part.

Theorem
.

It holds

\nu\left([\tilde{r}]:\exists r\in[\tilde{}\gamma]\text{ with }r_{0}=1_{\Gamma}\text{ and }\frac{L(r_{n})}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right)\xrightarrow[n\mapsto\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

Indeed, denote
\hat{R}_{n}(c)
the measure on the left hand side, and

H_{n}(c)=\hat{\nu}\left(\left\{\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}}\in\widetilde{Y}:\frac{L(h(\mathchoice{\underline{\displaystyle x}}{\underline{\textstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptstyle x}}{\underline{\scriptscriptstyle x}})_{n})}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}\leq c\right\}\right).

By the previous Corollary,
\lim_{n}H_{n}(c)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
. On the other hand, Lemma
5.7
in
[
73
]
shows the existence of some
K>0
so that for every
n\geq 1,c\in\mathbb{R}
:
H_{n}(c)\leq\hat{R}_{n}(c)\leq H_{n}(c+\frac{K}{\sigma\sqrt{n}})
. As a consequence,
\lim_{n}\hat{R}_{n}(c)=\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{c}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sigma}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

The second part of
Corollary

D
follows from the same skeleton of reasoning as above, this time following section
8
of
[
33
]
, which is based on
[
76
]
. The only difference is that one substitutes Theorem
3.8
of that article by
Proposition

9.32
. We refer the reader to the above cited works for more details.
